Cycles of Well-Linked Sets I: an Elementary Bound for Directed Cycle Packing Thanks: The results in this manuscript were also presented in Milani’s PhD thesis [ 25 ] . A preliminary version of this paper was published at FOCS 2024 [ 16 ] .
Meike Hatzel Technical University Darmstadt, Germany research@meikehatzel.com Stephan Kreutzer Technische Universität Berlin, Germany stephan.kreutzer@tu-berlin.de Marcelo Garlet Milani National Institute of Informatics, Tokyo, Japan research@mgarletmilani.com Irene Muzi Universität Hamburg, Germany irene.muzi@gmail.com

Abstract
In 1996, Reed, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas [Combinatorica 1996] proved Younger’s Conjecture, which states that, for all directed graphs D D , there exists a function f f such that, if D D does not contain k k disjoint cycles, then D D contains a feedback vertex set, i.e. a subset of vertices whose deletion renders the graph acyclic, of size bounded by f ⁡ ( k ) f(k) . However, the function obtained by Reed, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas in their paper is enormous and, in fact, not even elementary. We prove the first elementary upper bound for the function f f above, showing it is upper-bounded by a power tower of height 8. Our proof is inspired by the breakthrough result of Chekuri and Chuzhoy [J. ACM 2016], who proved a polynomial bound for the Excluded Grid Theorem for undirected graphs. We translate a key concept of their proof to directed graphs by introducing paths of well-linked sets (PWS) , and show that any digraph of large directed treewidth contains a large PWS, which in turn contains a large fence. We believe that the theoretical tools developed in this work may find applications beyond the results above, in a similar way as the path-of-sets-system framework due to Chekuri and Chuzhoy [J. ACM 2016] did for undirected graphs (see, for example, Hatzel, Komosa, Pilipczuk and Sorge [Discret. Math. Theor. Comput. Sci. 2022], Chekuri and Chuzhoy [SODA 2015] and Chuzhoy and Nimavat [arXiv 2019]). Indeed, in a follow-up paper, we apply this framework to improve the bounds of the Directed Grid Theorem.



1
Introduction

The Erdős-Pósa Theorem
[
13
]
is one of the first results in structural graph theory considering the relationship between “packing” multiple copies of some structure in a graph and the size of a set “covering” all such copies.
More precisely, they proved that every undirected graph contains
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles or it contains a feedback vertex set, i.e. a subset of vertices whose deletion renders the graph acyclic, of size bounded by
\mathcal{O}(k\log{k})
.
They also prove that the function they obtained is tight up to constant factors.

The
packing-covering duality
above inspired a long line of research on similar results for several combinatorial objects.
We refer the reader to
[
29
]
for a survey on the topic and to
[
2
,
28
,
35
]
for more recent results.
For a (to the best of our knowledge) complete and up-to-date list, see
[
30
]
.

The validity of an analogous property for directed cycles was conjectured by Younger
[
36
]
in 1973 and proven by Reed, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas
[
32
]
in 1996.
However, the function obtained by
[
32
]
is very far from the bound found by Erdős and Pósa for undirected cycles and is, in fact, not even elementary.

The main result of this paper is a new proof of the result above due to
[
32
]
, but providing instead an elementary bound for the dependency between the maximum number of disjoint directed cycles and the size of a minimum feedback vertex set.
More precisely, we prove the following, where
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
is a
power tower
of height 8 which we define later.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
D
be a digraph.
Then
D
has
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles or there is some
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
of size at most
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
such that
D-X
is acyclic.

The proof in
[
13
]
relies on a theorem due to
[
11
, Theorem 4]
which states that
there is a constant
c_{3}
such that every graph on
n
vertices and
n+\ell
edges contains
c_{3}\cdot\ell/\log\ell
pairwise edge-disjoint cycles.

The proof of this statement, in turn, relies on another result from the same paper stating that
every graph of minimum degree 3 contains a cycle of at most
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
edges.

These statements are, however, not true for digraphs, as one can take an acyclic orientation of a clique as a counter-example to the former and the
cylindrical grid
of order
k
as a counter-example for the latter.
Hence, already showing the existence of some (not necessarily elementary) function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}
requires different techniques from those used in the undirected setting.

To overcome the issues above,
[
32
]
used a directed grid structure called a
fence
1
1

1

For subtle technical reasons, a fence
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
in our notation corresponds to a fence
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P})
in the notation of
[
32
,
21
]
.
to reroute paths in a digraph in order to close cycles.
More precisely, they prove the following, where

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
are polynomials defined later.

Lemma 1.2

(
[
S
]
tatement (3.1))
.

reed1996packing]

Let
k,p,q,r\geq 1
be integers.
Let
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
be a
(p,q)
-fence in a digraph
D
where
q\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
and
p\geq r
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be an
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
-
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
linkage of order
r\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
.
Then,
D
contains
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles.

Unfortunately, their method of obtaining a fence results in a non-elementary bound.
Additionally, they used a recursive argument.
As any super-polynomial step in such a recursion yields a non-elementary bound in the end, and as not all of our bounds are polynomial, we cannot use recursive arguments in the same way as they did.
Since we cannot follow the structure of their proof, we instead draw inspiration from
[
8
,
3
,
4
,
20
]
.

The starting point of our proof is the following result due to
[
3
,
4
]
.

Lemma 1.3

(
[
3
, Lemma 4.2]
,
[
4
, Lemma 4.3.1]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\leq w
. For each strongly connected digraph
H
, the digraph
D
either has
k
disjoint copies of
H
as a topological minor, or contains a set
T
of at most
k\cdot(w+1)
vertices such that
H
is not a topological minor of
D-T
.

By setting
H={\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{
{{}}
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{}{}{}
{}
{}{}{}
\lxSVG@fillstroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014pt2}
in the statement above, we obtain that digraphs of small directed treewidth either contain many pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles or admit a feedback vertex set of small size.
Hence, for our results, it suffices to show that digraphs of sufficiently large directed treewidth contain many pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles.

To this end, we follow the structure of the proof by
[
20
]
, which was split into roughly three parts:
First, they obtain a grid-like structure called a
web
.
Then, they construct a fence from this web where the “end” of the fence is well-linked back to its “beginning”.
Finally, they use such a
back-linkage
to close the cycles of the cylindrical grid.
We note that
[
32
]
also obtained their fence through a web, and, while the bounds of
[
20
]
are also non-elementary for all three parts, the overall structure of the latter proof is not recursive, making it more suitable for improvements towards elementary bounds.
Hence, our first technical contribution is obtaining a web in a given digraph whose directed treewidth is large enough but still upper-bounded by an elementary function of the size of the web (
section

5
).

To obtain a fence from the web, we draw inspiration from the
path-of-sets system
framework due to Chekuri and Chuzhoy
[
8
]
, which played an important role in their proof of a polynomial bound for the (undirected) Grid Theorem.
In rough terms, a path-of-sets system describes a sequence of highly connected sets organised in a path-like structure, a concept which can be adapted to the directed setting.
In order to handle all the cases that appear in the directed setting, we need to consider two types of highly connected sets, namely
well-linked
and
order-linked
sets.
This, in turn, leads us to our definitions of
paths of well-linked sets
,
paths of order-linked sets
and
cycles of well-linked sets
.
These three concepts naturally capture the connectivity properties provided by
fences
,
acyclic grids
and
cylindrical grids
, respectively.

As undirected connectivity is considerably different from its directed counterpart, it is unsurprising that most of the proof techniques of
[
8
]
cannot be easily adapted to the directed setting.
Thus, in order to obtain the connectivity properties required above, we develop a framework based on the concept of
temporal digraphs
(see, e.g.
[
6
,
26
]
for an overview of results on temporal graphs).
Informally, a temporal digraph is a sequence of digraphs (on potentially different vertex or arc sets) where each digraph represents the state of the temporal digraph at a given point in time.
Walks in temporal digraphs need to respect the time at which each arc is available.
In our setting, temporal digraphs arise naturally when we consider disjoint paths intersecting a sequence of disjoint subgraphs in the same order.

One of our main contributions in this context is introducing a novel concept called
H
-routings
for digraphs and temporal digraphs, which is a weaker relation than strong immersions or butterfly minors for digraphs.
That is, if a digraph
D
contains
H
as a strong immersion or as a butterfly minor, then it also contains an
H
-routing.
A central part of our proof consists in establishing a relation between connectivity in digraphs and the existence of certain
H
-routings in temporal digraphs.
Using the framework above, we obtain one of our main results (the meaning of
A(S_{0})
and
B(S_{\ell})
is given later in
definition

8.1
).

Theorem
9.2
.

Every digraph
D
with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{short:thm:high_dtw_to_POSS_plus_back-linkage}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{7}(\operatorname{poly}^{25}(w,\ell))
contains a path of well-linked sets
(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},

S_{1},

\dots,

S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P})
of width
w
and length
\ell
such that
B(S_{\ell})
is well-linked to
A(S_{0})
in
D
.

Since we also show that a large path of well-linked sets contains a large fence, by using
Lemmas

1.2
and
9.2
, we can solve the large directed treewidth case of
theorem

1.1
, completing the proof of our main result.

Theorem

also plays an important role in obtaining an elementary bound for the Directed Grid Theorem.
Intuitively, a digraph consisting of a fence
F
with an internally disjoint linkage
\mathcal{L}
from the “end” of
F
back to its beginning is very close to a cylindrical grid.
In
[
17
]
, we prove that one can indeed find such a disjoint linkage
\mathcal{L}
starting from a path of well-linked sets as obtained in
Theorem

.

Our modular approach facilitates the transfer of the intermediate results in our proof to other settings.
Well-linked sets play an important role in several results in the theory of digraphs (for example, in
[
32
,
19
,
10
,
20
]
), and our framework provides additional tools for obtaining such sets.

From an algorithmic perspective, our intermediate concepts allow us to divide the problem of finding an acyclic grid in a digraph into subproblems such as finding long walks in temporal digraphs, constructing
H
-routings and obtaining well-linked sets.
These subproblems can be studied independently of each other, simplifying the process of identifying bottlenecks and computational obstacles for the original problem.

The paper is organised as follows.

Sections

2
and
3
contain preliminary definitions used throughout the paper.
In
section

4
, we provide an overview of the proof.
We construct a web in a digraph of large directed treewidth in
section

5
, improving the corresponding step of the proof of
[
20
]
from a non-elementary to an elementary bound.
Our framework on temporal digraphs is introduced in
section

6
, where we also obtain the
H
-routings from which we construct our order-linked and well-linked sets.
In
sections

7
and
8
, we introduce the concepts of paths of order-linked sets and paths of well-linked sets, respectively, and show how to obtain the corresponding grid type from each of them.
In
section

9
, we apply the framework developed in the sections above in order to construct a path of well-linked sets, which we then use to obtain the disjoint cycles, completing the proof of our main result,
theorem

1.1
.
Finally, we present our final remarks and open questions in
section

10
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we establish our notation and recall standard concepts and results from the literature used throughout the paper.

Sequences, sets and functions.

Given sequences
S_{1}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j})
and
S_{2}\coloneqq(y_{1},y_{2},\dots
,
y_{k})
, we write
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}
for the sequence
S_{3}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j},y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k})
.
We say that
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}
is a
decomposition
of
S_{3}
.
The following is a well-known theorem about sequences of numbers due to Erdős and Szekeres.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
14
]
)
.

Let
r,s\in\mathds{N}
.
Every sequence of distinct numbers of length at least
\mathchoice{\left(r-1\right)}{(r-1)}{(r-1)}{(r-1)}\mathchoice{\left(s-1\right)}{(s-1)}{(s-1)}{(s-1)}+1
contains a monotonically increasing subsequence of length
r
or a monotonically decreasing subsequence of length
s
.

An
ordered set
is a sequence
A=\mathchoice{\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\right)}{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})}{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})}{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})}
such that all elements of
A
are distinct.
The order
\leq_{A}:A\times A
induced by
A
is defined by
a_{i}\leq_{A}a_{j}
for all
1\leq i\leq j\leq k
.
An
ordered subset

A^{\prime}\subseteq A
then is just a subsequence of
A
, that is, the order of the elements is preserved.
If we obtain an ordered set
A^{\prime}
from a set
A
by fixing an order, we call
A^{\prime}
an
ordering
of
A
.

Power towers and polynomials

Let
d
be an integer and
V=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
a set of variables.
A
polynomial
of degree
d
over
V
is a function
p(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
of the form
p(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{e_{j,i}})
, where for each
1\leq i\leq n
and each
1\leq j\leq k
we have that
c_{i}\in\mathds{R}
,
e_{j,i}\in\mathds{N}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{k}e_{j,i}\leq d
.
We write
\operatorname{poly}^{d}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
for the set of all functions
f
for which there is a polynomial
p
of degree
d
over the variable set
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}
such that
f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{O}(p(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}))
.

We define
power towers
as follows.
Given an integer
h
and a set of functions
F
over a set of variables
V
, we define a set of functions
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(F)
recursively as follows.
We set
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{0}(F)=F
and define
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(F)
as
\{f:\mathds{R}^{|V|}\to\mathds{R}\mid f\in\mathcal{O}(2^{g(V)}),g\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h-1}(F)\}
for
h>1
.
If
F=\operatorname{poly}^{d}(V)
, we say that a function
f\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(F)
is a
power tower
of height
h
.

Graphs and digraphs.

We denote by
E(G)
the edge/arc set of a graph
G
, directed or not, and by
V(G)
its vertex set. We often use
G
for undirected and
D
for directed graphs (often referred to as
digraphs
).

Let
D
be a digraph.
Given a set of vertices
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, we write
D-X
for the digraph
(Y\coloneqq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\setminus X
,
E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\cap(Y\times Y))
.
Similarly, given a set of arcs
F\subseteq E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, we write
D-F
for the digraph
(V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)},E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\setminus F)
.

If
D
is a digraph and
v\in V(D)
, then
N^{\mathsf{in}}_{D}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mid(u,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\}
is the set of
in-neighbours
and
N^{\mathsf{out}}_{D}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mid(v,u)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\}
the set of
out-neighbours
of
v
.
By
\mathsf{indeg}(D){v}\coloneqq|N^{\mathsf{in}}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}|
we denote the
in-degree
of
v
and by
\mathsf{outdeg}(D){v}\coloneqq|N^{\mathsf{out}}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}|
its out-degree.
When working with a set or another structure
X
containing digraphs, we write
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(X\right)}{(X)}{(X)}{(X)}
to mean the digraph obtained by taking the union of all digraphs in
X
.

Paths and walks.

A
walk
of length
\ell
in a digraph
D
is a sequence of
vertices
W\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\right)}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}
such that

\mathchoice{\left(v_{i},v_{i+1}\right)}{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, for all
0\leq i<\ell
.
We write
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
for
v_{0}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
for
v_{\ell}
and say that

W
is a
v_{0}
-
v_{\ell}
-walk.

A walk
W\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\right)}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}
is called a
path
if no vertex appears twice in it and it is called a
cycle
if
v_{0}=v_{\ell}
and
v_{i}\neq v_{j}
for all
0\leq i<j<\ell
.

We often identify a walk
W
in
D
with the corresponding subgraph and write
V(W)
and
E(W)
for the set of vertices and arcs appearing on it.

Given two walks
W_{1}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j})
and
W_{2}\coloneqq(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k})
with
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W_{1}\right)}{(W_{1})}{(W_{1})}{(W_{1})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W_{2}\right)}{(W_{2})}{(W_{2})}{(W_{2})}
, we make use of the concatenation notation for sequences and write
W_{1}\cdot W_{2}
for the walk
W_{3}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j},y_{2},y_{3},\dots,y_{k})
.
We say that
W_{1}\cdot W_{2}
is a decomposition of
W_{3}
.
If
W_{1}
or
W_{2}
is an empty sequence, then the result of
W_{1}\cdot W_{2}
is the other walk (or the empty sequence if both walks are empty).

Let
P
be a path in a digraph
D
and let
X
be a set of vertices with
V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\cap X\neq\emptyset
.
We consider the vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
of
P
ordered by their occurrence on
P
.
Let
i
be the highest index such that
p_{i}\in X
and let
j
be the smallest index such that
p_{j}\in X
.
We call
p_{i}
the
last vertex of
P
in
X
or, depending on the perspective, the last element of
X
on
P
, and
p_{j}
the
first vertex of
P
in
X
or the first vertex of
X
on
P
.

Specific digraphs.

We denote the digraph of a path on
k
vertices by
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
.
For the
bidirected path on
k
vertices, we write
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}\coloneqq(\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}\},\{(u_{i},u_{j})\mid 1\leq i,j\leq k\text{ and }|i-j|=1\})
.
The
cycle on
k
vertices
is given by
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014ptk}\coloneqq(\{u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{k-1}\},\{(u_{i},u_{i+1\mod k})\mid 0\leq i<k\})
.
Finally, we write
{\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.50691pt}}_{k}\coloneqq(\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}\},\{(u_{i},u_{j})\mid 1\leq i,j\leq k\text{ and }i\neq j\})
for the
complete digraph on
k
vertices
.

Connectivity.

A digraph
D
is said to be
strongly connected
if for every
u,v\in V
there is a
u
-
v
-path
and
a
v
-
u
-path in
D
.
We say
D
is
unilateral
if for every
u,v\in V
there is a
u
-
v
-path
or
a
v
-
u
-path in
D
.
Finally,
D
is
weakly-connected
if the underlying undirected graph of
D
is connected.

A
feedback vertex set
of
D
is a set
X\subseteq V(D)
such that
D-X
is acyclic.

Linkages and separators.

Let
A,B\subseteq V(D)
.
An
A
-
B
-walk is a walk
W
that starts in
A
and ends in
B
.
A set
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
is an
A
-
B
-separator
if there are no
A
-
B
-paths in
D-X
.

A
linkage
in
D
is a set
\mathcal{L}
of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths.
The
order

|\mathcal{L}|
of
\mathcal{L}
is the number of paths it contains.

An
A
-
B
-linkage
of order
k
is a linkage
\mathcal{L}\coloneqq\{L_{1},L_{2},\dots,L_{k}\}
such that
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\in A
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\in B
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
We write
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
for the set
\{\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\mid L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}\}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
for the set
\{\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\mid L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}\}
.
We also extend the notation for path concatenation to linkages.
Given linkages
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k}\}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k}\}
such that
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
, we write
\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{Q}
for the linkage
\{P_{a}\cdot Q_{b}\mid P_{a}\in\mathcal{P},Q_{b}\in\mathcal{Q}\text{ and }\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(P_{a}\right)}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{b}\right)}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}\}
.

It is often convenient to use a linkage
\mathcal{L}
as a function
\mathcal{L}:\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\to\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
.
The expression
\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\left(a\right)}{(a)}{(a)}{(a)}=b
then means that
\mathcal{L}
contains a path starting in
a
and ending in
b
.

We frequently use the following classical result by Menger
[
24
]
.

Theorem 2.2

(Menger’s Theorem
[
24
]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph,
A,B\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
with
|A|=|B|
.
There is an
A
-
B
-linkage of size
k
in
D
if and only if every
A
-
B
-separator has size at least
k
.

Throughout the paper, we frequently work with a special kind of linkage that we define next.

Definition 2.1

(minimal linkage)
.

Let
D
be a digraph,
H\subseteq D
be a subgraph and
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage of order
k
.

\mathcal{L}
is
minimal with respect to
H
, or
H
-minimal
, if for all arcs
e\in\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}}E\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
there is no
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-linkage of order
k
in the graph
(\mathcal{L}\cup H)-e
.

Given a linkage
\mathcal{L}
in a digraph
D
and a subgraph
H\subseteq D
, we can always obtain a linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
with same order and same endpoints as
\mathcal{L}
which is
H
-minimal by iteratively removing arcs
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
for which a
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-linkage of order
|\mathcal{L}|
avoiding
e
exists.

Minimal linkages were used extensively in
[
20
]
.
The idea is that, when constructing paths of an
H
-minimal linkage
\mathcal{L}
, we always prefer to use arcs of
H
over arcs not in
E(H)
.
This implies the following property, which we exploit frequently in our proofs.

Definition 2.2

(weak minimality)
.

A linkage
\mathcal{L}
in a digraph
D
is
weakly
k
-minimal
with respect to a subgraph
H
of
D
if for every
L_{1}\cdot e\cdot L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
with
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
there is a
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
-separator of size at most
k-1
in
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.

Observation 2.3
.

Let
H
be a subgraph of a digraph
D
and let
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage which is
H
-minimal.
Then
\mathcal{L}
is weakly
|\mathcal{L}|
-minimal with respect to
H
.

Proof 2.3
.

Assume towards a contradiction that there is some
L\in\mathcal{L}
and some
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
such that
L
can be decomposed into
L_{1}\cdot e\cdot L_{2}
and there is no
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
-separator of size less than
|\mathcal{L}|
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.
By
theorem

2.2
, there is a
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
-linkage
\mathcal{Q}
of order
|\mathcal{L}|
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.

Let
S
be a minimum
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-separator in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.
Because
\mathcal{L}
is
H
-minimal, we have that
|S|<|\mathcal{L}|
.
Hence,
S
must hit every path in
\mathcal{L}\setminus\{L\}
and must be disjoint from
L
.

Since
|\mathcal{Q}|=|\mathcal{L}|
, there is some
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
which is not hit by
S
.
Hence, there is a
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}
-path in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e-S
, a contradiction to the assumption that
S
is a separator.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}
is weakly
|\mathcal{L}|
-minimal with respect to
H
.

We close this part by recalling the definition of well-linkedness, an important property of a central concept in our proof, the path of well-linked sets.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
A,B
be sets of vertices in a digraph
D
.
We say that
A
is well-linked to
B
in
D
if for every
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and every
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
with
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|
there is an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage of order
|A^{\prime}|
in
D
.
If
A
is well-linked to
A
, then we say that
A
is a
well-linked set
.

Minors.

Given a digraph
D
and an arc
(u,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, we say that
(u,v)
is
butterfly contractible
if
\mathsf{outdeg}(){u}=1
or
\mathsf{indeg}(){v}=1
.
The
butterfly contraction
of
(u,v)
is the operation which consists in removing
u
and
v
from
D
, then adding a new vertex
uv
, together with the arcs
\{(w,uv)\mid w\in\mathsf{indeg}(D){u}\}
and
\{(uv,w)\mid w\in\mathsf{outdeg}(D){v}\}
.
Note that, by definition of digraphs, we
remove
duplicated arcs and loops, that is, arcs of the form
(w,w)
.
If there is a subgraph
D^{\prime}
of
D
such that we can construct another digraph
H
from
D^{\prime}
using butterfly contractions, then we say that
H
is a
butterfly minor of
D
, or that
D
contains
H
as a butterfly minor
.

3
Directed Treewidth and Grids

As mentioned before,
lemma

1.3
allows us to focus on the case that the given digraph has large directed treewidth.
For this reason, in this section, we recall directed treewidth and the dual concepts of brambles and cylindrical grids.
We also define various other forms of “grids” in directed graphs used in the sequel.

Directed treewidth was originally introduced by Reed
[
33
]
and by Johnson, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas
[
19
]
(see also
[
18
]
).
Adler
[
1
]
showed that the original definition in
[
19
]
of directed treewidth is not closed under butterfly minors.
We, therefore, use the variant of directed treewidth defined in
[
21
]
, which is closed under taking butterfly minors.

An
arborescence

T
is an acyclic directed graph obtained from an undirected rooted tree by orienting all edges away from the root.
That is,
T
has a vertex
r_{0}
, called the root of
T
, with the property that for every
r\in V(T)
there is a unique directed path from
r_{0}
to
r
in
T
.
For each
r\in V(T)
, we denote the subarborescence of
T
induced by the set of vertices in
T
reachable from
r
by
T_{r}
.
In particular,
r
is the root of
T_{r}
.

Definition 3.1

(
[
21
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

A
directed tree-decomposition
of a digraph
D
is a triple
(T,\beta,\gamma)
, where
\beta:V(T)\to 2^{V(D)}
and
\gamma:E(T)\to 2^{V(D)}
are functions and
T
is an arborescence such that

(T1)

\{\beta(t):t\in V(T)\}
is a partition of
V(D)
into (possibly empty) sets and

(T2)

for every
e=(s,t)\in E(T)
, there is no closed directed walk in
D-\gamma(e)
containing a vertex in
A
and a vertex in
B
, where
A=\bigcup\{\beta(t):t\in V(T_{t})\}
and
B=V(D)\setminus A
.

For
t\in V(T)
we define
\Gamma(t)\coloneqq\beta(t)\cup\bigcup\{\gamma(e):e\sim t\}
, where
e\sim t
if
e
is incident to
t
, and we define
\beta(T_{t})\coloneqq\bigcup\{\beta(t):t\in V(T_{t})\}
.
The
width
of
(T,\beta,\gamma)
is the smallest integer
w
such that
|\Gamma(t)|\leq w+1
for all
t\in V(T)
.
The
directed treewidth
of
D
is the smallest integer
w
such that
D
has a directed tree-decomposition of width
w
.
The sets
\beta(t)
are called the bags and the sets
\gamma(e)
are called the guards of the directed tree-decomposition.

A natural dual to directed tree-decompositions are objects called
brambles
.
The concept of brambles was also introduced by
[
19
]
.
For the same reason as before, we use the variant of brambles defined in
[
20
]
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
bramble
in a digraph
D
is a set
\mathcal{B}
of strongly connected subgraphs
B\subseteq D
such that
B\cap B^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
for all
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
.

A
cover
of
\mathcal{B}
is a set
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
of vertices such that
V(B)\cap X\neq\emptyset
for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
.
Finally, the
order
of a bramble
\mathcal{B}
is the minimum size of a cover for
\mathcal{B}
.
The bramble number
bn(D)
of
D
is the maximum order of a bramble in
D
.

We also need the following relation between brambles and directed treewidth.
It can be obtained from results due to
[
19
]
by converting brambles to havens and back, and the statement was proven formally by
[
22
]
.

Lemma 3.3

(
[
22
, Corollary 6.4.24]
)
.

There are constants
c,c^{\prime}
such that for all digraphs
D
,
\operatorname{bn}(D)\leq c\cdot\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\leq c^{\prime}\cdot\operatorname{bn}(D)
.

By combining the statement (1.1) of
[
19
]
and Lemma 6.4.20 of
[
22
]
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.4

(
[
19
,
22
]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph.
If
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq 2k
, then
D
contains a bramble of order
k
.

We now define another obstruction to directed treewidth called
cylindrical grids
,
which are illustrated in
figure

1
.

Definition 3.5
.

A
cylindrical grid
of order
k
is a digraph
G_{k}
consisting of
k
pairwise disjoint directed cycles
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{k}
of length
2k
, together with a set of
2k
pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2k}
of length
k-1
such that

\blacktriangleright

each path
P_{i}
has exactly one vertex in common with each cycle
C_{j}
and both endpoints of
P_{i}
are in
V\mathchoice{\left(C_{1}\right)}{(C_{1})}{(C_{1})}{(C_{1})}\cup V\mathchoice{\left(C_{k}\right)}{(C_{k})}{(C_{k})}{(C_{k})}
,

\blacktriangleright

the paths
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2k}
appear on each
C_{i}
in this order, and

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq 2k
, if
i
is odd, then the cycles
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{k}
occur on
P_{i}
in this order and, if
i
is even, then the cycles occur in the reverse order
C_{k},C_{k-1},\dots,C_{1}
.

Besides cylindrical grids, several different ways of defining “directed grids” have been considered in the literature (for example
[
32
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
20
]
).
Two of these, called
acyclic grids
and
fences
(see
figure

2
), are used at various points in our proof.
Since we are interested in grids in the context of minors, we define grids by linkages instead of giving explicit vertex and arc sets.

To motivate the following definitions, let us dissect a cylindrical grid
((C_{1},\dots,C_{k}),(P_{1},\dots,

P_{2k}))
as follows.
An important difference between cylindrical grids and grids in undirected graphs is that cylindrical grids are
locally acyclic
in the following sense.
Suppose we delete in each cycle
C_{i}
the arc
e_{i}
whose head is on the path
P_{1}
.
The dotted red lines mark these arcs in
figure

1
.
The resulting digraph is acyclic and consists of two linkages: the linkage
\{P_{1},\dots,P_{2k}\}
and the linkage
\{C_{1}-e_{1},\dots,C_{k}-e_{k}\}
which contains for each cycle
C_{i}
the path that remains once the arc
e_{i}
is deleted.
Digraphs of this form are called
fences
.
See
figure

1
for a drawing of cylindrical grids illustrating how they are constructed from a fence with additional arcs closing the cycles.

Definition 3.6
.

A
(p,q)
-fence
is a tuple
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{P}=\mathchoice{\left(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p}\right)}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p})}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\right)}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}
are linkages,

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq 2p
and each
1\leq j\leq q
, the digraph
P_{i}\cap Q_{j}
is a path (and therefore non-empty),

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq j\leq q
, the paths
P_{1}\cap Q_{j},P_{2}\cap Q_{j},\dots,P_{2p}\cap Q_{j}
appear in this order along
Q_{j}
, and

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq 2p
, if
i
is odd then the paths
P_{i}\cap Q_{1},P_{i}\cap Q_{2},\dots,P_{i}\cap Q_{q}
appear along
P_{i}
in this order, and if
i
is even instead, then the paths
P_{i}\cap Q_{q},P_{i}\cap Q_{q-1},\dots,P_{i}\cap Q_{1}
appear in this order along
P_{i}
.

Figure 1
:
Cylindrical grid
G_{8}
of order
8
drawn in two ways.
The drawing on the right illustrates how a cylindrical grid is obtained from a fence.
The
dotted orange
paths symbolise the arcs
e_{i}
that close the cycles drawn solid on the left.

See
figure

2(b)
for an illustration.
The “horizontal” paths, or
rows
, constitute the linkage
\mathcal{Q}
and the
columns
form the linkage
\mathcal{P}
.

A useful property of a
(p,q)
-fence
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
is that if
A\subseteq\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
and
B\subseteq\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
are sets with
|A|=|B|\leq p
, then there is an
A
-
B
-linkage
\mathcal{L}
of order
|A|
in the digraph
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
.

Now, let us further decompose the fence constructed from the cylindrical grid to obtain an even simpler form of directed grid.
In a fence, we can only route from “left to right” and we can route “upwards” as well as “downwards”.
An even simpler form of a directed grid is obtained if we remove the “upwards” paths from a fence, i.e. every second column.
The resulting digraph is called an
acyclic grid
, illustrated in
figure

2(a)
.

Definition 3.7
.

An
acyclic
(p,q)
-grid
is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{P}=\mathchoice{\left(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p}\right)}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\right)}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}
are linkages,

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq p
and each
1\leq j\leq q
, the digraph
P_{i}\cap Q_{j}
is a path (and therefore non-empty),

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq j\leq q
, the paths
P_{1}\cap Q_{j},P_{2}\cap Q_{j},\dots,P_{p}\cap Q_{j}
appear in this order along
Q_{j}
, and

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq p
, the paths
P_{i}\cap Q_{1},P_{i}\cap Q_{2},\dots,P_{i}\cap Q_{q}
appear in this order along
P_{i}
.

(a)

An acyclic
(8,8)
-grid.

(b)

An
(8,8)
-fence.

Figure 2
:
An acyclic grid and a fence.

Acyclic grids only allow routing from top to bottom and from left to right.

Another grid-like structure we define is the
web
, initially introduced by Reed et al. in
[
32
]
.
Webs are essential in the proof of the Directed Grid Theorem in
[
20
]
and are equally crucial for our results.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
D
be a digraph.
Two linkages
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{V}
in
D
constitute an
\mathchoice{\left(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|\right)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
if every path in
\mathcal{V}
intersects every path in
\mathcal{H}
.

The set
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is called the
top
of the web, while the set
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is called the
bottom
of the web.
Finally,
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is
well-linked
if
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is well-linked linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
in
D
.

Our proof uses an intermediate object called a
semi-web
.
Unlike a web, we do not require from a semi-web
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
that the paths in
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{V}
pairwise intersect each other.
Instead, a semi-web has a
degree
which controls how much the two linkages must intersect.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
D
be a digraph.
Two linkages
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{V}
in
D
form a
\mathchoice{\left(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|\right)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}
-
semi-web

\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
of degree
d
if every path in
\mathcal{V}
intersects at least
d
paths in
\mathcal{H}
.

Finally,
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is
well-linked
if
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
in
D
.

An
(h,v)
-web
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
of avoidance
d
from
[
20
]
corresponds to a
(h,v)
-semi-web
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
of degree
|\mathcal{H}|\cdot\frac{d-1}{d}
where
\mathcal{H}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{V}
in our notation.
The main reason for this modification is to avoid fractional calculations when determining the bounds of our functions.

4
Proof overview

Because
lemma

1.3
handles the small directed treewidth case, we focus on the case where the given digraph has large directed treewidth.
In particular, we are interested in finding specific types of
paths of sets
, as defined below.

4.1
Paths of sets

Our first main result consists in obtaining an acyclic grid whose end is well-linked back to its beginning, inside which we can easily find a fence.
To achieve this, we first construct objects with similar connectivity properties to these two types of grids without necessarily being planar.

It is a simple exercise to verify that, in a sufficiently large fence,
the “left side” is well-linked to the “right side” (see
figure

2(b)
for the meaning of “left” and “right” used here).
Hence, the notion of well-linked sets is the connectivity property that we want in our abstraction of fences.
Acyclic grids, however, provide a different type of connectivity.
To capture this, we introduce the notion of
r
-
order-linkedness
,
which we formally define later in
definition

7.2
.

We can now define the concepts of
paths of well-linked sets
, which behave like fences, and of
paths of
r
-order-linked sets
, which behave like acyclic grids.

Definition
7.3
and
8.1

(path of
r
-order-linked
/
well-linked
sets)
.

A
path of
r
-order-linked
/
well-linked
sets
of width
w
and length
\ell
is a tuple
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{S}
is a sequence of
\ell+1
pairwise disjoint subgraphs
\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell}\right)}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})},
which are called
clusters
,

\blacktriangleright

for every
0\leq i\leq\ell
there are disjoint
ordered
sets
A(S_{i}),B(S_{i})\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(S_{i}\right)}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}
of size
w
such that
A(S_{i})
is
r
-order-linked
/
well-linked
to
B(S_{i})
in
S_{i},

\blacktriangleright

\mathscr{P}
is a sequence of
\ell
pairwise disjoint linkages
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}
such that, for every
0\leq i<\ell,

\mathcal{P}_{i}
is a
B(S_{i})
-
A(S_{i+1})
-linkage of order
w
which is internally disjoint from
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
as well as disjoint from every
S\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{S_{i},S_{i+1}\}.

Further, a
path of
r
-order-linked sets

\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
is called
uniform
if for all
0\leq i<\ell
and for all
b_{1},b_{2}\in B(S_{i})
we have that
b_{1}\leq_{B(S_{i})}b_{2}
implies
\mathcal{P}_{i}(b_{1})\leq_{A(S_{i+1})}\mathcal{P}_{i}(b_{2}).

A
path of well-linked sets
is called
strict
if every vertex in
S_{i}
lies on an
A(S_{i})
-
B(S_{i})
-path.

We obtain the following connection between paths of order-linked sets and paths of well-linked sets, allowing us to focus on obtaining paths of order-linked sets when proving our main result.
The construction is quite similar to the one used to obtain a fence from an acyclic grid, and the bounds we obtain are essentially the same.

Lemma
8.3
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{short:proposition:order-linked to path of well-linked sets}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\coloneqq w(\ell+1)
.
Every path of
w
-order-linked sets
(\mathcal{S}
,
\mathscr{P})
of width at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{short:proposition:order-linked to path of well-linked sets}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}
and length at least
\ell
contains a path of well-linked sets
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})}
of width
w
and length
\ell
.

The following two statements allow us to construct acyclic grids and fences from the above objects.
Hence, for our first main result, it suffices to show that digraphs of large directed treewidth contain a large path of well-linked sets where the last cluster is well-linked to the first.

Theorem
7.2
.

There is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm-abbr:order_linked_to_acyclic_grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{7}(k))
such that every path of
1
-order-linked sets of width at least
w=k^{2}-1
and length at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm-abbr:order_linked_to_acyclic_grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
contains an acyclic
(k,k)
-grid.

Theorem
8.1
.

There are functions
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}\in\mathcal{O}(p^{5}q^{5})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{15}(p,q))
such that every path of well-linked sets
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
of width at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm-abbr:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}
and length
\ell\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm-abbr:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}
contains a
(p,q)
-fence.
Moreover,
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}\subseteq A(S_{0})
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}\subseteq B(S_{\ell})
.

This allows us to divide our proof into roughly two main “parts”.

The first part (
section

4.2
) consists in finding a well-linked web
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
from a bramble of high order where
\mathcal{P}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
.
We emphasise that the requirement of minimality is what makes obtaining such a web very challenging.

After obtaining the well-linked web, we can get a similar structure in which one linkage is “ordered” according to the other, constructing objects which are called
splits
and
segmentations
in
[
20
]
.

In the second part (
section

4.3
), we construct a path of well-linked sets from the splits and segmentations obtained previously with the additional property that the
B
-set of the last cluster is well-linked to the
A
-set of the first cluster.

Theorem
9.2
.

Every digraph
D
with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{short:thm:high_dtw_to_POSS_plus_back-linkage}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{7}(\operatorname{poly}^{25}(w,\ell))
contains a path of well-linked sets
(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},

S_{1},

\dots,

S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P})
of width
w
and length
\ell
such that
B(S_{\ell})
is well-linked to
A(S_{0})
in
D
.

Finally, we apply results from
[
32
,
3
]
in order to obtain our final main result.

Theorem
1.1
.

There is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{8}(\operatorname{poly}^{43}(k))
such that the following holds.
Let
D
be a digraph.
Then
D
has
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles or there is some
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
of size at most
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
such that
D-X
is acyclic.

4.2
Splits and segmentations

The construction of our paths of well-linked sets and paths of order-linked sets starts with
splits
and
segmentations
,
which in turn are obtained from webs by ensuring that one linkage of the web intersects the other in an ordered fashion.

The proof of
[
21
]
for obtaining a web works by starting with a
bramble
of high order and then showing that the existence of such a bramble implies the existence of an object called a
path-system
.
2
2

2

Despite the similarity in names, the path-systems we use here and the path-of-sets systems of
[
8
]
are unrelated mathematical objects.

The intuition behind the path-system is the following.
Say we want to construct a bidirected clique as a butterfly minor.
Towards this end, we take several linkages connecting different subsets.
Of course, these linkages might not be pairwise disjoint.
What we want from our path-system is the property that,
if the linkages we obtained above happen to be mostly disjoint from each other,
and if they intersect the path-system in a “clean” way,
then we should be able to easily construct our bidirected clique
(and, in particular, find many disjoint cycles).
The interesting and more challenging part of the proof lies in showing that,
if we fail at finding such a
clean path-system

or if we fail at getting the linkages to be disjoint,
then we can obtain one of two useful structures called
split
and
segmentation
.

We define path-systems (
definition

5.5
), splits and segmentations (
definition

5.1
) later.

Lemma

5.9
below essentially corresponds to Lemma 4.7 from
[
21
]
, and our proof is based on theirs.
Our proof relies on a result known as
Lovász Local Lemma

[
12
,
34
]
,
which allowed us to improve the bounds from non-elementary to polynomial functions.

Lemma
5.9
.

There are two polynomials
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1}\right)}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}\in\mathcal{O}(\ell_{2}+d_{1}p_{2})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(q_{1},p_{2}\right)}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}\in\mathcal{O}(q_{1}(p_{2})^{3})
such that the following holds.
Let
d_{1},q_{1},p_{2},\ell_{2}
be integers and
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A})
be an
\ell
-linked path-system of order
p
.
If
\ell\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1}\right)}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}
and
p\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(q_{1},p_{2}\right)}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}
, then
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}{(\mathcal{S})}{(\mathcal{S})}{(\mathcal{S})}
contains one of the following

(C1)

a well-linked
\mathchoice{\left(\ell,q_{1}\right)}{(\ell,q_{1})}{(\ell,q_{1})}{(\ell,q_{1})}
-semi-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})}
of degree
d_{1}
where
\mathcal{P}_{1}\in\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, or

(C2)

a clean
\ell_{2}
-linked path-system
(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})
of order
p_{2}
.

Using further results from
[
21
]
, we can prove the main result of this section.

Theorem
5.5
.

There is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{state:high-dtw-to-segmentation-or-split}}}\mathchoice{\left(x,y,q,k\right)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{5}(\operatorname{poly}^{5}(x,y,q,k))
such that the following holds.
Let
D
be a digraph.
Let
k,x,y,q
be integers.
If
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{state:high-dtw-to-segmentation-or-split}}}\mathchoice{\left(x,y,q,k\right)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}
, then
D
contains one of the following

(D1)

a cylindrical grid of order
k
as a butterfly minor,

(D2)

a
(y,q)
-split
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}
of some pair
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})
in
D
, where
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}
, or

(D3)

an
(x,q)
-segmentation
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
of some pair
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})
in
D
, where
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})}
.

As a cylindrical grid of order
k
contains
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles, the outcome
(D1)
above is sufficient for our results.
We still require this stronger property rather than just providing cycles, since we need it to improve the bounds of the Directed Grid Theorem in
[
17
]
.

4.3
Obtaining a path of well-linked sets

Several steps of our proofs revolve around linkages which intersect certain subgraphs in an ordered fashion.
To simplify our arguments and reasoning and to avoid repetition, we abstractly model this configuration with the help of the concept of
temporal digraphs
.

A
temporal digraph
is a pair
T=(V,\mathcal{A})
consisting of a vertex set
V
and sequence of arc sets
\mathcal{A}=\mathchoice{\left(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell}\right)}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}
such that
D_{t}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(V,A_{t}\right)}{(V,A_{t})}{(V,A_{t})}{(V,A_{t})}
is a digraph for all
1\leq t\leq\ell.

We also refer to
D_{t}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
as
layer
t
of
T
and call
t
a
time step
.
The
lifetime
of
T
is given by
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\coloneqq\ell.

A
temporal walk
of length
n
from
v_{0}
to
v_{n}
in a temporal digraph
T
is a sequence
W\coloneqq(v_{0},t_{0}),(v_{1},t_{1}),\ldots,(v_{n},t_{n})
such that
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in A_{t_{i}}
and
t_{i}<t_{i+1}\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1.

If such a walk exists, we say that
v_{0}

temporally reaches

v_{n}.

A temporal walk is said to be a
temporal path
if no vertex appears twice in the sequence.
Finally, we say that
W

departs
at
t_{0}
and
arrives
at
t_{n},
and that
t_{n}-t_{0}
is the
duration
of
W.

Usage of temporal digraphs arises naturally in a directed setting.
Consider the example given in
figure

3
of a temporal digraph obtained from a linkage
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\{P_{a},P_{b},P_{c}\}
passing through three digraphs
\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}
.
If we want to construct a new linkage starting and ending in a subset of the starting and endpoints of
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\{P_{a},P_{b},P_{c}\}
, then as soon as a path in our new linkage visits a vertex in
Q_{2}
, it can no longer use vertices from
Q_{1}
, as we only have connectivity from “left” to “right”.
Further, as we want some way of ensuring that our paths are disjoint and form a linkage, we are interested in the connectivity each
Q_{i}
provides “between” the paths in
\mathcal{P}
without intersecting other paths in
\mathcal{P}
.
This intuition leads us to the following definition.

Figure 3
:
The layers
D_{j}(T)
of the temporal graph
T\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}\right)}{(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})}{(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})}{(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})}
constructed from the graphs
Q_{j}
displayed above as defined in
Definition

.

Definition
6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a linkage and let
\mathcal{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\}
be a set of pairwise disjoint digraphs where each path
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
can be partitioned as
P_{i}^{1}\cdot P_{i}^{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot P_{i}^{q}=P_{i}
such that
V(P_{i}^{j})\cap V\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{j}\right)}{(Q_{j})}{(Q_{j})}{(Q_{j})}
for all
1\leq j\leq q.

The
routing temporal digraph
\mathchoice{\left(V,\mathcal{A}\right)}{(V,\mathcal{A})}{(V,\mathcal{A})}{(V,\mathcal{A})}
of
\mathcal{P}
through
\mathcal{Q}
, which we also refer to as
\mathscr{T}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}
, is constructed as follows.
We set
V=\mathcal{P}
and for each
1\leq j\leq q
we define
A_{j}=\{(P_{a},P_{b})\mid P_{a},P_{b}\in\mathcal{P}
and there is a path from
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{a}\right)}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}
to
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{b}\right)}{(P_{b})}{(P_{b})}{(P_{b})}
inside
Q_{j}
which is internally disjoint from
\mathcal{P}
\}
.

In our application of routing temporal digraphs, we want to translate paths (or more general structures we construct) within a routing temporal digraph
T
of a linkage
\mathcal{P}
through
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq\{Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q}\}
into corresponding subgraphs of
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
.
This is made possible by our requirement that a temporal walk in
T
only uses at most one arc in each layer.
For, if
W
is a temporal walk
W
in
T
and
e=(P_{i},P_{j})\in E\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
is an arc in layer
A_{l}
, say, then we can replace
e
by a path
L(e)\subseteq Q_{l}
connecting
P_{i}
to
P_{j}
.
As
W
contains at most one arc per layer, the paths
L(e)
and
L(e^{\prime})
are pairwise disjoint for distinct arcs
e\neq e^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the walk
W
naturally translates into a walk in
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
.

As the example in
figure

3
demonstrates, this would no longer work if temporal walks were allowed to use more than one arc per layer.
For instance, the digraph
Q_{2}
induces the arcs
(P_{c},P_{b})
and
(P_{b},P_{a})
in the routing temporal digraph
T_{2}
of
\mathcal{P}
through
\{Q_{2}\}
, but the walk
(P_{c},P_{b},P_{a})
in
T_{2}
does not correspond to any
P_{c}
-
P_{a}
-walk in
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup Q_{2}
.

Routings.

We define a containment relation for digraphs to better describe the kind of connectivity that a routing temporal digraph provides between the paths of the original linkage.
We point out to the reader that connectivity in temporal digraphs is not transitive.
Hence, restricting the following definition to arcs is not sufficient.

Definition
6.4
.

Let
H
be a digraph,
D
be a
(temporal)
digraph and
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}.

An
H
-routing (over
S
)
is a bijection
\varphi:V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}\to S
such that for each
u
-
v
-path
P
in
H
we can find a
(temporal)

\varphi(u)
-
\varphi(v)
-path in
D
which is disjoint from
S\setminus\varphi\mathchoice{\left(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\right)}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}.

See
section

6.1
for a more detailed discussion of this new containment relation and how it relates to other known ones.

In order to apply the framework defined above, we simplify the notions of splits and segmentations to slightly more general structures we call
ordered
and
folded
webs.

Definition
9.1
.

Let
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
be an
\mathchoice{\left(h,v\right)}{(h,v)}{(h,v)}{(h,v)}
-web.
We say that
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is an
ordered web
if there is an ordering of
\mathcal{V}=\mathchoice{\left(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v}\right)}{(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v})}{(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v})}{(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v})}
for which each path
H\in\mathcal{H}
can be decomposed into
H=H_{1}\cdot H_{2}\cdots H_{v}
such that
H_{i}
intersects
V_{j}
if and only if
i=j
.

Definition
9.8
.

An
(h,v)
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is a
folded web
if every
V_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
can be split as
V_{i}^{a}\cdot V_{i}^{b}\coloneqq V_{i}
such that both
V_{i}^{a}
and
V_{i}^{b}
intersect all paths of
\mathcal{H}
.

It is not difficult to show that a
(p,q)
-segmentation
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
from
[
21
]
is also an ordered web
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
, and that a
(p,q)
-split
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
is a folded ordered
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P})
web.

Our main results on routing temporal digraphs are about finding
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

,

and
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

-routings in different contexts.
First, we show that every unilateral temporal digraph with a long enough lifetime contains a walk with many vertices.
The reason why we consider temporal digraphs in which the layers are unilateral is that, given an ordered web
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
, each layer of
\mathscr{T}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is unilateral.

Lemma
6.15
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{short:lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}\coloneqq kn\sum_{i=1}^{kn}n^{i}
.
Let
T
be a temporal digraph with
n
vertices where each layer is unilateral, and let
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
be a set of size
k
.
If
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{short:lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}
, then
T
contains a temporal walk
W
with
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
.

We can use the walk obtained in
lemma

6.15
to construct a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routing.

Theorem
6.7
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{short:theorem:one-way connected temporal digraph contains P_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}\coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{short:lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2}\right)}{(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2})}{(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2})}{(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2})}
.
Let
T
be a temporal digraph in which every layer is unilateral.
If
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{short:theorem:one-way connected temporal digraph contains P_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}
and

such that

contains a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routing over

Intuitively, a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routing in a routing temporal digraph
\mathscr{T}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}
provides connectivity between the paths in
\mathcal{P}
which is similar to a column in an acyclic grid, which in turn is related to the concept of order-linkedness defined before.
This intuition is formalised below.

Lemma
7.6
.

Let

be integers.

be the routing temporal digraph of some linkage

-routing, then

is

-order-linked to

in

It is not difficult to see that, given a folded ordered web

Intuitively, due to these two types of routings providing connectivity in both directions between the paths of our linkage

, we can use them to obtain well-linked instead of order-linked sets.

Theorem
6.8
.

Let
T
be a temporal digraph such that
D_{i}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
is strongly connected for all

with

such that

contains an

-routing over

for some

This allows us to infer the following
proposition
, stating that if we can find a large enough linkage giving rise to a temporal digraph with long enough lifetime, then we can always find a sublinkage whose start-vertices are well-linked to its end-vertices.

Proposition
6.32
.

Let
k
be an integer,
h\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{2}(k))
,
D
be a digraph,
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage of order
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\mathcal{O}(k^{11})
in
D
and let
T
be the routing temporal digraph of
\mathcal{L}
through
\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\{H_{1},\dots,H_{h}\}
, where each
H_{i}
is a subgraph of
D
.
If each layer
D_{i}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
is strongly connected, then there exists some
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
of order
k
such that
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}
.

This, in turn, allows us to obtain a path of well-linked sets from a large enough folded ordered web.
There are functions
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{lemma:folded-web-to-pows}}}\mathchoice{\left(w\right)}{(w)}{(w)}{(w)}\in\mathcal{O}(w^{11})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{v}_{\ref{lemma:folded-web-to-pows}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{2}(w,\ell))
such that the following statements hold.

.

Let
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}

We can manipulate the path of well-linked sets given by

above in order to ensure that its extremities are contained in the extremities of

This will be useful in the proof of

Lemma

.

There are functions
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{state:ordered-web-to-well-linked-pows}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\in\mathcal{O}(w^{2}\ell^{2})

and length

.

A(S_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}
