We can now use the results above to prove
Theorem

.

Proof 4.1

(Proof sketch of
Theorem

)
.

By
theorem

5.5
, we have one of three cases.
If
D
contains a cylindrical grid of order
w\ell
, it contains a path of well-linked sets of width
w
and length
\ell
.

If
D
contains a
(y,q)
-split
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})
, then this split is essentially a folded ordered web
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
.
By
Corollary

, we can construct a path of well-linked sets from this split.
Moreover, the beginning and the end of this path of well-linked sets coincide with
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
, respectively.
As
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
, the path of well-linked sets obtained satisfies the restrictions in the statement.

In the last case,
D
contains an
(x,q)
-segmentation
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
, which also means that
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
is an ordered web.
Applying
lemma

9.6
to
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})
yields a path of well-linked sets.
Moreover, the beginning and the end of this path of well-linked sets coincide with
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}
, respectively.
As
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{H})}
, the path of well-linked sets obtained satisfies the restrictions in the statement.

To obtain our final main result, we first need a statement from
[
32
]
.

Lemma
1.2

(
[
32
, Statement (3.1)]
)
.

There are polynomials
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
such that the following holds.
Let
k,p,q,r\geq 1
be integers.
Let
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
be a
(p,q)
-fence in a digraph
D
where
q\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
and
p\geq r
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be an
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
-
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
linkage of order
r\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{statement:fence-plus-back-linkage-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
.
Then,
D
contains
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles.

Together with our previous results,
lemma

1.2
allows us to handle the case where the digraph has large directed treewidth.

Lemma
9.16
.

There is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{statement:high-dtw-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{8}(\operatorname{poly}^{43}(k))
such that the following holds.
If a digraph
D
has
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{statement:high-dtw-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
, then
D
contains
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles.

For the small directed treewidth case, we need the following statement from
[
3
]
.

Lemma
1.3

(
[
3
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\leq w
.
For every strongly connected digraph
H
, the digraph
D
either has
k
disjoint copies of
H
as a topological minor, or contains a set
T
of at most
k\cdot(w+1)
vertices such that
H
is not a topological minor of
D-T
.

We can now conclude with our final main result.

Theorem
1.1
.

There is a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{8}(\operatorname{poly}^{13}(k))
such that the following holds.
Let
D
be a digraph.
Then
D
has
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles or there is some
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
of size at most
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{f}_{\ref{statement:elementary-younger}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
such that
D-X
is acyclic.

Proof 4.2
.

Let
t_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{statement:high-dtw-implies-cycles}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
.
Let
H={\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014pt2}
.
If
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\leq t_{1}-1
, then, by
lemma

1.3
,
D
contains
k
vertex-disjoint copies of
H
as a topological minor or
D
contains a set
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
of size at most
kt_{1}
such that

does not contain
H
as a topological minor.
By choice of

, both cases imply the initial statement, and we are done.

Otherwise,
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq t_{1}
.
By
lemma

9.16
,
D
contains
k
pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles.

5
Constructing splits and segmentations

The construction of our paths of well-linked sets and paths of order-linked sets starts with
splits
and
segmentations
(see
definition

5.1
), which add more structure to webs by ensuring that one linkage of the web intersects the other in an ordered fashion.
We repeat below the definition of splits and segmentations by Kawarabayashi and Kreutzer
[
21
]
.

Definition 5.1

(
[
21
, Definitions 5.6 and 5.7]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
be linkages and let
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
be a sublinkage of order
q
.
Let
r\geq 0
.

(X1)

An
(r,q^{\prime})
-split of
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
(with respect to
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
)
is a pair
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
of linkages of order
r=|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|
and
q^{\prime}=|\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}|
with
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
such that there is a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
and edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{r-1}\in E(P)\setminus E(\mathcal{Q}^{\star})
such that
P=P_{1}e_{1}P_{2}\dots e_{r-1}P_{r}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\coloneqq(P_{1},\dots,P_{r})
and every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
can be divided into subpaths
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{r}
such that
Q=Q_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}\dots e^{\prime}_{r-1}Q_{r}
, for suitable edges
e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e^{\prime}_{r-1}\in E(Q)
, and
\emptyset\neq V(Q)\cap V(P_{i})\subseteq V(Q_{r+1-i})
, for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

(X2)

A subset
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
of order
q^{\prime}
is called a
q^{\prime}
-
segmentation
of
P\in\mathcal{P}
(with respect to
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
) if there are edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{q^{\prime}-1}\in E\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}^{\star}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\star})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\star})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{\star})}
with
P=P_{1}e_{1}\dots P_{q^{\prime}-1}e_{q^{\prime}-1}P_{q^{\prime}}
, for suitable subpaths
P_{1},\dots,P_{q^{\prime}}
, such that
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
can be ordered as
(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q^{\prime}})
and
V(Q_{i})\cap V(P)\subseteq V(P_{i})
.

(X3)

An
(r,q^{\prime})
-segmentation
(with respect to
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
) is a pair
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
where
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a linkage of order
r
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a linkage of order
q^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a
q^{\prime}
-segmentation (with respect to
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
) of every path
P_{i}
into segments
P^{i}_{1}e_{1}P^{i}_{2}\dots e_{q^{\prime}-1}P^{i}_{q^{\prime}}
.

(X4)

A segmentation
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
is
ordered
if for all
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
the order
\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q^{\prime}}\right)}{(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q^{\prime}})}{(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q^{\prime}})}{(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q^{\prime}})}
given by the
q^{\prime}
-segmentation of
P_{i}
is the same.
We say that
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
is an (ordered)
(r,q^{\prime})
-segmentation of
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
if
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
and every path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a subpath of a path in
\mathcal{P}
.

An
(r,q)
-split
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
or an
(r,q)
-segmentation
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
is well-linked if
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
.

One can obtain splits and segmentations from weakly-minimal webs using the following result.
We observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:y-split or x-segmentation}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q,x,y,c\right)}{(p,q,x,y,c)}{(p,q,x,y,c)}{(p,q,x,y,c)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{2}(\operatorname{poly}^{4}(p,q,x,y,c))
.

Lemma 5.2

(
[
20
, Lemma 5.13]
)
.

Let
p,q,q^{\prime},r,s,c,x,y\geq 0
be integers such that
p\geq x
and
q^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:y-split or x-segmentation}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q,x,y,c\right)}{(p,q,x,y,c)}{(p,q,x,y,c)}{(p,q,x,y,c)}\coloneqq(pq(q+c))^{2^{(x-1)y+1}}
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:y-split or x-segmentation}}}

.
If
D
contains a
(p,q^{\prime})
-web
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
where
\mathcal{P}
is weakly
c
-minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
, then
D
contains one of the following:

(S1)

a
(y,q)
-split
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
of
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
, or

(S2)

an
(x,q)
-segmentation
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
of
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{W}
is well-linked in
D
, then so is
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
.

With a simple pigeon-hole principle argument, it is possible to construct an ordered segmentation from a segmentation.
The proof provided below is based on some steps of the proof of Lemma 5.19 from
[
20
]
.

Observation 5.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
be a
(p,q)
-segmentation.
If
p\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{lemma:ordered x-segmentation}}}\mathchoice{\left(k,q\right)}{(k,q)}{(k,q)}{(k,q)}\coloneqq(k-1)q!+1
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{lemma:ordered x-segmentation}}}

, then there is
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
such that
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q})
is an ordered
(k,q)
-segmentation.

Proof 5.3
.

For each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
there is an ordering
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
of
\mathcal{Q}
witnessing that
\mathcal{Q}
is a
q
-segmentation of
P_{i}
.
There are at most
q!
distinct orderings
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.
Hence, by the pigeon-hole principle, there is some
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of size
k
such that
\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{j}
for all
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

Since the bounds for
lemma

5.2
are already elementary, in the remainder of this section we focus on obtaining a web while ensuring all the functions that arise are elementary.

We thereby improve upon the results of
[
21
]
, who show that digraphs of large directed treewidth contain a large
well-linked web
or a large cylindrical grid.
The bounds they obtain in this step are, however, non-elementary.
In particular, their proof uses an
iterated Ramsey
argument, and so the bounds obtained are a power tower whose height depends on
h,v
and
k
(where
h,v
and
k
are defined as in
theorem

5.2
).

Theorem 5.3

(
[
21
, Theorem 4.2 + Lemma 3.6 + Lemma 4.10]
)
.

Let
h,v,k\in\mathds{N}
.
There exists a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{thm:dtw_to_web}}}:\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\to\mathds{N}
such that every digraph
D
with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{thm:dtw_to_web}}}\mathchoice{\left(h,v,k\right)}{(h,v,k)}{(h,v,k)}{(h,v,k)}
contains a cylindrical grid of order
k
as a butterfly minor or a
\mathchoice{\left(h,v\right)}{(h,v)}{(h,v)}{(h,v)}
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
where
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is a well-linked set in
D
and
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{V}
-minimal.

To achieve an elementary bound, we utilise a result known as
Lovász Local Lemma
,
obtaining even a polynomial bound for a previously non-elementary step.
In total, however, the gap between directed treewidth and the size of the ordered web we can guarantee is upper bounded by a super-polynomial function.
Recall that
e=2.71828\ldots
is
Euler’s number
.

Lemma 5.4

(Lovász Local Lemma
[
12
,
34
]
)
.

Consider a set
\mathcal{E}
of events such that for each
A\in\mathcal{E}

\blacktriangleright

\mathsf{Pr}{[A]}\leq p<1
, and

\blacktriangleright

A
is mutually independent of a set of all but at most
d
other events.

If
ep(d+1)<1
, then with positive probability, none of the events in
\mathcal{E}
occur.

While
lemma

5.4
above is not constructive,
[
27
]
provided a randomised algorithm and
[
7
]
provided a deterministic algorithm for finding an assignment of the random variables which avoids all events
\mathcal{E}
.

The proof of
[
21
]
for obtaining a web works by starting with a
bramble
of high order and then showing that the existence of such a bramble implies the existence of an object called a
path-system
.
We repeat the definition of path-system below (see
figure

4
for an illustration).

Definition 5.5

(path-system
[
21
]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph and let
\ell,p\geq 1
.
An
\ell
-linked path-system of order
p
is a sequence
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A})
, where

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\big(A_{i}^{\mathsf{in}},A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}\big)_{1\leq i\leq p}
such that
|A_{i}^{\mathsf{in}}|=|A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}|=\ell
and
A\coloneqq\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq p}A_{i}^{\mathsf{in}}\cup A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}\subseteq V(D)
is a well-linked set of order
2\ell p
, for all
1\leq i\leq p
,

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{P}\coloneqq(P_{1},\dots,P_{p})
is a sequence of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths and for all
1\leq i\leq p
,
A_{i}^{\mathsf{in}},A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}\subseteq V(P_{i})
and all
v\in A^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}
occur on
P_{i}
before any
v^{\prime}\in A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}
and the first vertex of
P_{i}
is in
A_{i}^{\mathsf{in}}
and the last vertex of
P_{i}
is in
A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}
and

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{L}\coloneqq(L_{i,j})_{1\leq i\not=j\leq p}
is a sequence of linkages such that for all
1\leq i\not=j\leq p
,
L_{i,j}
is a linkage of order
\ell
from
A_{i}^{\mathsf{out}}
to
A_{j}^{\mathsf{in}}
.

The path-system
\mathcal{S}
is
clean
if
Q\cap P_{s}=\emptyset
holds for all pairwise distinct
i,j,s\in\{1,2,\ldots,p\}
and all
Q\in L_{i,j}
.

A_{1}^{\mathsf{in}}
A_{1}^{\mathsf{out}}
A_{2}^{\mathsf{out}}
P_{1}
P_{2}
P_{3}
L_{1,3}
L_{1,2}
L_{3,1}
L_{3,2}
L_{2,1}
L_{2,3}
A_{2}^{\mathsf{in}}
A_{3}^{\mathsf{in}}
A_{3}^{\mathsf{out}}

Figure 4
:
A clean
2
-linked path-system of order 3.

A path-system can be obtained from a bramble using the following lemma.
We define
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{k}_{\ref{state:bramble-contains-linked-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(\ell,p\right)}{(\ell,p)}{(\ell,p)}{(\ell,p)}=(4\ell p)(2\ell p+1)
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{k}_{\ref{state:bramble-contains-linked-path-system}}}

and observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{k}_{\ref{state:bramble-contains-linked-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(\ell,p\right)}{(\ell,p)}{(\ell,p)}{(\ell,p)}\in\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2}p^{2})
.

Lemma 5.6

(
[
21
, Lemma 4.6]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph and let
\ell,p\geq 1
.
If
D
contains a bramble of order
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{k}_{\ref{state:bramble-contains-linked-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(\ell,p\right)}{(\ell,p)}{(\ell,p)}{(\ell,p)}
, then
D
contains an
\ell
-linked path-system
\mathcal{S}
of order
p
.

5.1
Semi-webs

Our proof uses an intermediate object called a
semi-web
.
Unlike a web, we do not require from a semi-web
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
that the paths in
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
pairwise intersect each other.
Instead, a
semi-web
has a degree controlling the intersections between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
.

A web can be obtained from a semi-web using
lemma

5.7
below.
Towards this end, we use
Observations

5.3
and
5.4
, which summarise some basic properties of a linkage
\mathcal{L}
which is minimal with respect to another linkage
\mathcal{P}
.

Observation 5.3

(
[
21
, Lemma 2.14]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph.
Let
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L}
be linkages such that
\mathcal{L}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{P}
.
Then
\mathcal{L}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for every
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

Observation 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{Q}
be two linkages such that
\mathcal{P}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
If
P
does not intersect any path in
\mathcal{Q}
, then
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
.

We adapt the following statement from
[
21
]
to our notation, while also fixing some minor mistakes in their proof.
We first define

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{state:semi-web-to-web}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{state:semi-web-to-web}}}\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime},q,k\right)}{(p^{\prime},q,k)}{(p^{\prime},q,k)}{(p^{\prime},q,k)}

\displaystyle=qk(p^{\prime})^{k}.

Lemma 5.7

(
[
21
, Lemma 4.10]
)
.

Let
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})}
be a
\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\right)}{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}
-semi-web of degree
k
in a digraph
D
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.
If
q^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{state:semi-web-to-web}}}\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime},q,k\right)}{(p^{\prime},q,k)}{(p^{\prime},q,k)}{(p^{\prime},q,k)}
, then
D
contains a well-linked
\mathchoice{\left(p_{1},q\right)}{(p_{1},q)}{(p_{1},q)}{(p_{1},q)}
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}
where

\mathcal{P}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
,

\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,

\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and

p_{1}\geq k
.

Proof 5.8
.

Define a function
f
as
f(Q)=\{P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mid V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(Q\right)}{(Q)}{(Q)}{(Q)}=\emptyset\}
for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.
By assumption,
|f(Q)|\leq p^{\prime}-k
for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

As there are
\binom{p^{\prime}}{|f(Q)|}
choices for each
f(Q)
, and as
\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{p^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}-k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{p^{\prime}}{k}\leq k(p^{\prime})^{k}
, by the pigeon-hole principle there is some
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
of order
q
such that
X\coloneqq f(Q_{a})=f(Q_{b})
holds for all
Q_{a},Q_{b}\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\setminus X
and let
p_{1}=|\mathcal{P}|
.
Note that
p_{1}\geq k
.
By
Observations

5.3
and
5.4
,
\mathcal{P}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
.
Hence,
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}
is a
(p_{1},q)
-web where
\mathcal{P}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
, as desired.

Lemma

5.9
essentially corresponds to Lemma 4.7 from
[
21
]
, and our proof is based on theirs.
The idea is to attempt to construct a semi-web from some linkage
L_{a,b}\in\mathcal{L}
and some
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
If we do not find any semi-web, it means that the linkages in
\mathcal{L}
are mostly disjoint from
\mathcal{P}
.
We then use this observation to argue that, for each pair
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, there are only few other paths
P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}
which are “bad” for the choice
P_{i},P_{j}
, that is, we cannot easily construct a clean path-system if we take
P_{i},P_{j}
and
P_{r}
.
This allows us to construct our “bad” events in order to apply Lovász Local Lemma.

We define

\displaystyle d^{\prime}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2}\right)}{(p_{2})}{(p_{2})}{(p_{2})}

\displaystyle=3(p_{2})^{2}/2-15p_{2}/2+10,

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1}\right)}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}

\displaystyle=\ell_{2}+(p_{2}-2)d_{1},

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(q_{1},p_{2}\right)}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}

\displaystyle=(2e(d^{\prime}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2}\right)}{(p_{2})}{(p_{2})}{(p_{2})}+1)q_{1}+1)p_{2}.

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1}\right)}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}\in\mathcal{O}(\ell_{2}+d_{1}p_{2})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(q_{1},p_{2}\right)}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}\in\mathcal{O}(q_{1}(p_{2})^{3})
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
d_{1},q_{1},p_{2},\ell_{2}
be integers.
Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A})
be an
\ell
-linked path-system of order
p
.
If
\ell\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1}\right)}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}{(p_{2},\ell_{2},d_{1})}
and
p\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:path-system-to-clean-path-system}}}\mathchoice{\left(q_{1},p_{2}\right)}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}{(q_{1},p_{2})}
, then
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S}\right)}{(\mathcal{S})}{(\mathcal{S})}{(\mathcal{S})}
contains one of the following

(C1)

a well-linked
\mathchoice{\left(\ell,q_{1}\right)}{(\ell,q_{1})}{(\ell,q_{1})}{(\ell,q_{1})}
-semi-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})}
of degree
d_{1}
where
\mathcal{P}_{1}\in\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, or

(C2)

a clean
\ell_{2}
-linked path-system
(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})
of order
p_{2}
.

Proof 5.10
.

Let
d=3(p_{2})^{2}/2-15p_{2}/2+10
and
p_{2}^{\prime}=\lceil 2eq_{1}(d+1)\rceil
+ 1.

First, if there is some
\mathcal{L}_{s,t}\in\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{s,t}
is not minimal with respect to
\mathcal{P}
, then replace such a linkage with another
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}_{s,t}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}_{s,t})}{(\mathcal{L}_{s,t})}{(\mathcal{L}_{s,t})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}_{s,t}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}_{s,t})}{(\mathcal{L}_{s,t})}{(\mathcal{L}_{s,t})}
-linkage of the same order which is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{P}
.
This does not alter the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is an
\ell
-linked path-system of order
p
.
Further, if
p_{2}=1
, we can trivially obtain a clean
\ell_{2}
-linked path-system satisfying
(C2)
by taking any path in
\mathcal{P}
and setting
\mathcal{L}=\emptyset
.
Hence, we can assume that
p_{2}\geq 2
.

Define a function
\gamma
as follows.
For each distinct
P_{s},P_{t}\in\mathcal{P}
we set (see
figure

5
for an illustration)

\displaystyle\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})=\{P\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{s},P_{t}\}\mid

\displaystyle\penalty\ P\text{ intersects at least $d_{1}$ paths of $\mathcal{L}_{s,t}$ or}

\displaystyle\penalty\ P\text{ intersects at least $d_{1}$ paths of $\mathcal{L}_{t,s}$}\}.

P_{s}
P_{t}
L_{s,t}
L_{t,s}
\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})=\gamma(P_{t},P_{s})

Figure 5
:
Illustration of the set
\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})
used in the proof of
lemma

5.9
, given in blue.
This set consists of the paths of
\mathcal{P}
which intersect many paths in at least one of the linkages between
P_{s}
and
P_{t}
.

If there is a pair of distinct
P_{s},P_{t}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
|\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})|\geq 2q_{1}
, then we construct our pair
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})
as follows.
By the pigeon-hole principle, there is a choice of
\mathcal{P}_{1}\in\{\mathcal{L}_{s,t},\mathcal{L}_{t,s}\}
and a set
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\subseteq\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})
of size
q_{1}
such that every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
intersects at least
d_{1}
paths of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
By assumption,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.
Furthermore,
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}_{1}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}_{1}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}{(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}
.
Hence,
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})
satisfies
(C1)
.

We now assume that
|\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})|<2q_{1}
holds for all distinct
P_{s},P_{t}\in\mathcal{P}
.
We construct a set
\mathcal{P}_{2}
as follows.

First, distribute the elements of
\mathcal{P}
arbitrarily into
p_{2}
disjoint sets
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{p_{2}}
, each of size
p_{2}^{\prime}
.
For each
\mathcal{X}_{i}
, define a random variable
x_{i}
which corresponds to sampling one element of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
from a uniform distribution.

For each three distinct
a,b,c\in\{1,2,\ldots,p_{2}\}
, let
A_{a,b,c}
be the event that
x_{a}\in\gamma(x_{b},x_{c})
.

Since the event
A_{a,b,c}
depends only on the values of
x_{a},x_{b}
and
x_{c}
, we know that
A_{a,b,c}
is independent from
A_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}}
if
\{a,b,c\}\cap\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\}=\emptyset
.
Hence,
A_{a,b,c}
is independent from all but at most
\binom{p_{2}}{3}-\binom{p_{2}-3}{3}=d
other events.

We now bound the value of
\mathsf{Pr}{[A_{a,b,c}]}
.
As there are
(p_{2}^{\prime})^{3}
distinct choices for the tuple
(x_{a},x_{b},x_{c})
and for each choice of
x_{b},x_{c}
there are at most
2q_{1}
choices of
x_{a}
such that
x_{a}\in\gamma(x_{b},x_{c})
, we have that
\mathsf{Pr}{[A_{a,b,c}]}\leq(2q_{1}(p_{2}^{\prime})^{2})/(p_{2}^{\prime})^{3}=2q_{1}/p_{2}^{\prime}
.

Because
p_{2}^{\prime}\geq e\cdot(d+1)\cdot 2q_{1}+1
, from
lemma

5.4
we know that the probability that none of the events
A_{a,b,c}
occur is positive.
That is, there is some choice of
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p_{2}}
such that
x_{a}\not\in\gamma(x_{b},x_{c})
for all three distinct
a,b,c\in\{1,2,\ldots,p_{2}\}
.
We set
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p_{2}}\}
.

For each distinct
P_{s},P_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
define
\mathcal{L}_{s,t}^{\prime}=\{L\in\mathcal{L}_{s,t}\mid{}\text{for all $P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\setminus\{P_{s},P_{t}\}$ we have}

V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\cap L=\emptyset\}
.

By choice of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we have that
P_{r}\not\in\gamma(P_{s},P_{t})
holds for all pairwise distinct
P_{s},P_{t},P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
Hence,
|\mathcal{L}_{s,t}^{\prime}|\geq|\mathcal{L}_{s,t}|-(p_{2}-2)d_{1}\geq\ell_{2}
.

Finally, choose
\mathcal{A}_{2}
as the elements
A_{i}^{in},A_{i}^{out}
of
\mathcal{A}
satisfying
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
Clearly,
(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2})
is a clean
\ell_{2}
-linked path-system of order
p_{2}
, satisfying
(C2)
.

We attempt to obtain a bidirected clique from a clean path-system by first iteratively trying to obtain disjoint paths inside
\mathcal{L}
pairwise connecting the paths of
\mathcal{P}
.
In case this construction fails, we obtain a well-linked semi-web.

5.2
Splits or segmentations with elementary bounds

As seen before in
lemma

5.7
, in order to obtain a web from a semi-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})}
of degree
d
, we require that
\mathcal{Q}
is much larger than
\mathcal{P}
.
Unfortunately, it is not possible to directly use the results of
[
21
]
to obtain the required web, as the sizes of the linkages
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
provided by their statements do not match.
Instead, we need to modify the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 4.8]
, ensuring that in each step of the iteration described at the end of the previous subsection we obtain a sufficiently large gap between
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
so that we can apply
lemma

5.7
.
Similarly, we again require a large gap between
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
when obtaining a split or segmentation from a web using
lemma

5.2
.
Hence, we need to “pay” the function from both
lemma

5.2
and
lemma

5.7
during each step of the iteration in our proof.

In order to avoid repetition, we only present here the part of the proof from
[
21
]
which we modify, which is
lemma

5.12
below.
The remainder of the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 4.8]
is given by
lemma

5.11
.

Lemma 5.11

(
[
21
, proof of Lemma 4.8]
)
.

Let
k
be an integer and let
\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A}\right)}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A})}{(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A})}
be a clean 1-linked path-system of order
p
in a digraph
D
such that all paths in
\mathcal{L}
are pairwise vertex-disjoint.
If
p\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:clean-and-disjoint-path-system-to-clique}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\coloneqq 3k
, then
D
contains a bidirected clique of order
k
as a butterfly minor.

The proof of
lemma

5.12
works by iteratively constructing pairwise disjoint paths
R_{r}
.
If in some step we cannot construct the desired path
R_{r}
, then we argue that some linkage
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}
in the clean path-system intersects many paths of some other linkage
\mathcal{L}_{s,t}
.
Our modifications of the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 4.8]
are essentially focused on ensuring that both linkages forming the semi-web described above are large enough for us to apply
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.2
.

We define

\displaystyle k^{\prime}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}

\displaystyle=2\binom{3k}{2},

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:clean-path-system-to-web-or-clique}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{p}_{\ref{state:clean-path-system-to-web-or-clique}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}

\displaystyle=3k,

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:clean-path-system-to-web-or-clique}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:clean-path-system-to-web-or-clique}}}\mathchoice{\left(x,y,q,k\right)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}

\displaystyle=\left(2xqk^{\prime}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\right)^{2^{k^{\prime}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\left(y\left(x-1\right)+1\right)}\left(3x\right)^{k^{\prime}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}}}.

Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{state:clean-path-system-to-web-or-clique}}}\mathchoice{\left(x,y,q,k\right)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{2}(\operatorname{poly}^{5}(x,y,q,k))
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Proof 5.13
.

Let
k_{1}=2\binom{3k}{2}
.
We define functions

and

We start by setting

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(k_{1})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{state:semi-web-to-web}}}\mathchoice{\left(x,q,x\right)}{(x,q,x)}{(x,q,x)}{(x,q,x)},

\displaystyle f(k_{1}+1)

\displaystyle=0,f(k_{1})=q^{\prime}(k_{1})+1,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(k_{1})

\displaystyle=x

and

\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}(k_{1})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:y-split or x-segmentation}}}\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime}(k_{1}),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(k_{1})\right)}{(p^{\prime}(k_{1}),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(k_{1}))}{(p^{\prime}(k_{1}),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(k_{1}))}{(p^{\prime}(k_{1}),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(k_{1}))}.

For

, we set

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=f(r+1)+x-1,

\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}(r)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:y-split or x-segmentation}}}\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r)\right)}{(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r))}{(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r))}{(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r))},

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{state:semi-web-to-web}}}\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime\prime}(r),x\right)}{(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime\prime}(r),x)}{(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime\prime}(r),x)}{(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime\prime}(r),x)}

and

\displaystyle f(r)

\displaystyle=(k_{1}-r+1)q^{\prime}(r)+1.

By repeatedly applying the functions above, we obtain the following recursive equality, which we use later

\displaystyle f(r)

\displaystyle=1+x\left(k_{1}-r+1\right)\left(x+f{\left(r+1\right)}-1\right)^{x}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left(q\left(x+f{\left(r+1\right)}-1\right)\left(x+q+f{\left(r+1\right)}-1\right)\right)^{2^{y\left(x-1\right)+1}}.

Before proceeding with the proof, we give upper bounds for the functions defined above.

Claim 1
.

For all

\displaystyle f(k_{1}-r)\leq\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{\left(r+1\right)\left(y\left(x-1\right)+1\right)}\left(3x\right)^{r+1}}

Proof 5.14

(Proof)
.

We prove the statement iteratively by starting at

.

\displaystyle f(k_{1})=x^{x+1}q+1\leq\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{3x}\leq\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{3x2^{y\left(x-1\right)+1}}.

We show that they also hold for

To aid readability, we replace

\displaystyle f(k_{1}-r)=x\left(r+1\right)\left(x+f{\left(k_{1}-r+1\right)}-1\right)^{x}

\displaystyle\quad{}\cdot\left(q\left(x+\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{rw}\left(3x\right)^{r}}\right)\left(x+q+\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{rw}\left(3x\right)^{r}}\right)\right)^{2^{w}}

\displaystyle(\text{induction})

\displaystyle\leq x\left(r+1\right)\left(2\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{rw}\left(3x\right)^{r}}\right)^{x}\left(4q\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{rw+1}\left(3x\right)^{r}}\right)^{2^{w}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{rw}\left(3x\right)^{r}}\right)^{x}\left(4k_{1}xq\left(2k_{1}xq\right)^{2^{rw+1}\left(3x\right)^{r}}\right)^{2^{w}}

\displaystyle 2^{rw}x\left(3x\right)^{r},

Hence, the claim follows by induction.

).

For each
r+1<q\leq k_{1}
, let
\mathchoice{\left(s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)}{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}{(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}=\sigma(q)
and choose an
A_{s^{\prime}}^{\text{out}}
-
A_{t^{\prime}}^{\text{in}}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{\sigma(q)}
of order
p^{\prime}(r+1)
inside
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(q)}\cup\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{s,t}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(q)}\cup\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{s,t})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(q)}\cup\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{s,t})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(q)}\cup\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{s,t})}
which satisfies
(L4)
such that every path in
\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{\sigma(q)}
has no inner vertex in
V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\cup\bigcup_{r^{\prime}=1}^{r}V\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(r^{\prime})}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(r^{\prime})})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(r^{\prime})})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(r^{\prime})})}
and which is
\mathcal{L}^{r+1}_{s,t}
-minimal (satisfying
(L3)
).
Thus,
(L1)
,
(L2)
,
(L3)
and
(L4)
hold for the step
r+1
.

Case 2:
For every
P_{z}\in\mathcal{L}^{r}_{i,j}
there is some
r<q_{z}\leq k_{1}
for which
P_{z}
intersects least
p^{\prime}(r)-f(r+1)+1=x
paths in
\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(q_{z})}
.

Let
\mathchoice{\left(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\right)}{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}=\sigma(q_{z})
.
As
|\mathcal{L}^{r}_{i,j}|=f(r)=(k_{1}-r-1)q^{\prime}(r)+1
, by the pigeon-hole principle there is a
r<w\leq k_{1}
and a
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{r}_{i,j}
of order
q^{\prime}(r)
such that all paths in
\mathcal{Q}
intersect at least
x
paths in
\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)}
.
Hence,
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}
is a
\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime}(r)\right)}{(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime}(r))}{(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime}(r))}{(p^{\prime}(r),q^{\prime}(r))}
-semi-web of degree
x
.
Finally, as the starting points and endpoints of both
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)}
lie in the well-linked set

, we have that
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}
is also a well-linked semi-web.

Applying

5.7
to
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\sigma(w)},\mathcal{Q})}
yields a well-linked
\mathchoice{\left(p_{2},q^{\prime\prime}(r)\right)}{(p_{2},q^{\prime\prime}(r))}{(p_{2},q^{\prime\prime}(r))}{(p_{2},q^{\prime\prime}(r))}
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})}{(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})}{(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})}
where
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is minimal with respect to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, where
p^{\prime}(r)\geq p_{2}\geq x
.
As
q^{\prime\prime}(r)\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:y-split or x-segmentation}}}\mathchoice{\left(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r)\right)}{(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r))}{(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r))}{(p^{\prime}(r),q,x,y,p^{\prime}(r))}
and

is also

-minimal with respect to

, we can apply

to
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})}{(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})}{(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})}
, obtaining two cases.

If

5.2
(S2)
holds, then we satisfy
(W3)
.
Otherwise,
lemma

5.2
(S1)
holds, satisfying
(W2)
.

If Case 2 above never occurs, then we obtain a sequence of pairwise disjoint paths
\mathcal{R}\coloneqq(R_{1},R_{2},\ldots,R_{k_{1}})
such that for all

, the path

is an

-

-path which is disjoint from

, where
\mathchoice{\left(i,j\right)}{(i,j)}{(i,j)}{(i,j)}=\sigma(r)
.
By

, we obtain a bidirected clique of size

as a butterfly minor, satisfying

.

We conclude this section by combining the main statements proven above, yielding the following theorem, which is used later on, both in this paper and in

]
.
The statement of the theorem is slightly stronger than what is required in this paper, as we only need the

disjoint cycles from the outcome

of

.
However, we need the statement in this form when improving the bounds of the Directed Grid Theorem.

We define

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{state:high-dtw-to-segmentation-or-split}}}\mathchoice{\left(x,y,q,k\right)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}{(x,y,q,k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{5}(\operatorname{poly}^{9}(x,y,q,k))
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let

Let

If

.

Proof 5.15

,

,

6
Temporal digraphs and routings

In our proof, we are frequently faced with problems of the following form.
We have already constructed two linkages
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
, say.
However, while the paths within the same linkage are disjoint by definition, a pair of paths from different linkages may intersect arbitrarily or not at all.
So the intersection pattern between the two linkages can be arbitrarily complex.
The problem then is to find a subgraph of a specific form in
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
.
Problems of this form occur frequently in this research area, including in the proof of the Directed Grid Theorem
[
20
]
.

In this section, we develop a framework based on temporal digraphs which allows us to formulate these problems more abstractly.
This abstraction allows us to simplify many arguments and unify proofs by isolating the core ideas common to several of these proofs.
Moreover, our framework allows us to obtain much better bounds and to prove elementary bounds for results that require non-elementary bounds in
[
20
]
.

There are several different definitions of temporal graphs and temporal walks, each applicable in a different context.
Here, we use the notation from
[
6
,
26
]
and adapt it to the directed setting.
We first define our notion of temporal digraphs and walks, and then discuss how they arise in our context.

Definition 6.1
.

A
temporal digraph
is a pair
T=(V,\mathcal{A})
consisting of a vertex set
V
and sequence of arc sets
\mathcal{A}=\mathchoice{\left(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell}\right)}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}
such that
D_{t}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(V,A_{t}\right)}{(V,A_{t})}{(V,A_{t})}{(V,A_{t})}
is a digraph for all
1\leq t\leq\ell
.
We also refer to
D_{t}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
as
layer
t
of
T
and call
t
a
time step
.
The
lifetime
of
D
is given by
\ell\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\coloneqq\ell
.

Next, we define paths and walks in the temporal setting.
A
temporal walk
in a temporal digraph
T
is required to obey the “timeline” of
T
, i.e. the order in which arcs occur on the walk must respect the time steps of
T
.
In our setting, we need a more restrictive definition that only allows a temporal walk to use at most one arc of each layer.

Definition 6.2
.

A
temporal walk
of length
n
from
v_{0}
to
v_{n}
in a temporal digraph
T
is a sequence
W\coloneqq(v_{0},t_{0}),(v_{1},t_{1}),\ldots,(v_{n},t_{n})
such that
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in A_{t_{i}}
and
t_{i}<t_{i+1}\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
.
If such a walk exists, we say that
v_{0}

temporally reaches

v_{n}
.
A temporal walk is said to be a
temporal path
if no vertex appears twice in the sequence.
Finally, we say that
W

departs
at
t_{0}
and
arrives
at
t_{n}
, and that
t_{n}-t_{0}
is the
duration
of
W
.

We often have situations where some linkage
\mathcal{P}
intersects pairwise disjoint digraphs
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{q}
.
If the paths in
\mathcal{P}
“agree” on the order of the digraphs
Q_{i}
, then we can construct an auxiliary temporal digraph in the following way.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a linkage and let
\mathcal{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\}
be a set of pairwise disjoint digraphs such that each path
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
can be partitioned as
P_{i}^{1}\cdot P_{i}^{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot P_{i}^{q}=P_{i}
such that
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{i}^{j}\right)}{(P_{i}^{j})}{(P_{i}^{j})}{(P_{i}^{j})}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{j}\right)}{(Q_{j})}{(Q_{j})}{(Q_{j})}
for all
1\leq j\leq q
.

The
routing temporal digraph
\mathchoice{\left(V,\mathcal{A}\right)}{(V,\mathcal{A})}{(V,\mathcal{A})}{(V,\mathcal{A})}
of
\mathcal{P}
through
\mathcal{Q}
is constructed as follows.
We set
V=\mathcal{P}
and for each
1\leq j\leq q
we define
A_{j}=\{(P_{a},P_{b})\mid P_{a},P_{b}\in\mathcal{P}
and there is a path from
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{a}\right)}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}
to
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{b}\right)}{(P_{b})}{(P_{b})}{(P_{b})}
inside
Q_{j}
which is internally disjoint from
\mathcal{P}
\}
.

We refer the reader to
figure

3
for an illustration of the definition above
and to
section

4.3
for a discussion of why routing temporal digraphs are defined precisely in this way.

6.1
H
-routings

Since the connectivity provided by each layer of a temporal digraph can differ considerably, we need some way to describe connectivity involving several layers.
We also want to describe connectivity between specific sets of vertices.
We are particularly interested in deciding when we can construct a path
R
between two paths
P_{1},P_{2}
such that
R
is disjoint from some specific set
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

The concept of
H
-routings
, given in
definition

6.4
below, allows us to use a digraph
H
in order to describe which connections we need.
Since both digraphs and temporal digraphs have a concept of paths, we can define
H
-routings for both digraphs and temporal digraphs analogously.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
H
be a digraph,
D
be a digraph or temporal digraph, and
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.
An
H
-routing (over
S
)
is a bijection
\varphi:V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}\to S
such that for each
v
-
u
-path
P
in
H
we can find a
\varphi(v)
-
\varphi(u)
-path (or temporal path, resp.) in
D
which is disjoint from
S\setminus\varphi\mathchoice{\left(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\right)}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}
.

Note that reachability in temporal digraphs is not transitive, as the example in
figure

3
demonstrates: in the temporal digraph
T_{1,3}\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}\},\{A_{1},A_{3}\}\right)}{(\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}\},\{A_{1},A_{3}\})}{(\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}\},\{A_{1},A_{3}\})}{(\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}\},\{A_{1},A_{3}\})}
containing only the layers
A_{1}
and
A_{3}
,
P_{a}
is reachable from
P_{c}
and
P_{b}
is reachable from
P_{a}
but
P_{b}
is not reachable from
P_{c}
.
To get a meaningful concept of
H
-routings, we therefore have to require that not only for every arc in
H
but also for every path in
H
there is a temporal path in
T
with the same start and endpoint.

Compared to other containment relations,
H
-routings are rather
weak
in the sense given by the three observations below.
We first recall the definitions of strong immersions and strong minors.

An immersion of a digraph
H
in a digraph
D
is a function
\varphi
from vertices and arcs of
H
such that

\blacktriangleright

\varphi(v)
is a vertex of
D
for each
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, and the restriction of φ to
V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
is injective.

\blacktriangleright

\varphi(a)
is a directed path (or cycle) in
D
for each
a∈E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, and if
b\in E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
is distinct from
a
, then
\varphi(a)
and
\varphi(b)
are arc-disjoint.

\blacktriangleright

If
a\in E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
has tail
u\in V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
and head
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, then
\varphi(a)
is a
\varphi(u)
-
\varphi(v)
-path, and if
a
is a loop, then
\varphi(a)
is a cycle passing through
\varphi(v)
.

An immersion
\varphi
is
strong
if it additionally satisfies the following condition:
If
a∈E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
is not incident with
v∈V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, then
φ(a)
does not contain
φ(v)
.
We say an immersion is
weak
if we do not require it to be strong.

Observation 6.1
.

If a digraph
D
contains
H
as a strong immersion, then it also contains an
H
-routing.

Proof 6.5
.

Let φ be an immersion of
H
in
D
and let
φ^{\prime}
be the restriction of φ to
V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.

By definition,
φ^{\prime}
is injective.
Further, for every arc
(u,v)∈E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, the
φ(u)
-
φ(v)
-path given by
φ((u,v))
witnesses the existence of a
φ^{\prime}(u)
-
φ^{\prime}(v)
-path in
D
which is disjoint from all vertices in the image of
φ^{\prime}
, except for
u
and
v
.
Hence,
φ^{\prime}
is an
H
-routing in
D
.

The statement above is not true if we take weak immersions instead, as illustrated in
figure

6
.

v_{0}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
D
:
u_{1}
u_{3}
u_{0}
u_{2}
H
:

Figure 6
:
The digraph
D
contains
H
as a weak immersion, as witnessed by the mapping
u_{i}\mapsto v_{i}
.
However, it does not contain an
H
-routing.

In order to define strong minors, we need to first define a digraph operation.
Let
D,H
be two digraphs.
A
strong contraction
of a strongly-connected component
X
of
D

is the operation of replacing
X
by a single vertex
x
and,
for each
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(D-X\right)}{(D-X)}{(D-X)}{(D-X)}
,
adding the arc
(v,x)
if
N^{\mathsf{out}}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\cap X\neq\emptyset
and
adding the arc
(x,v)
if
N^{\mathsf{in}}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\cap X\neq\emptyset
.

We say that
H
is a
strong minor
of
D

if we can obtain
H
from a subgraph of
D

through strong contractions.

Observation 6.2
.

If a digraph
D
contains
H
as a strong minor,
then it also contains an
H
-routing.

Proof 6.6
.

For each vertex in
v∈V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
,
let
V_{v}
be the set of vertices which were strongly contracted in
D

when constructing
H
as a strong minor.
Construct a function
φ:V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}\to V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}

by fixing some arbitrary
u_{v}\in V_{v}
for each
v∈V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
and
setting
φ(v)=u_{v}
.

Because strongly connected sets of vertices are contracted into a single vertex
during strong contractions, the sets
V_{v}
defined above are pairwise vertex-disjoint.
Hence, φ is injective.

Let
(u,v)∈E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
.
By definition of strong minors, there is some vertex
u^{\prime}\in V_{u}
and some
v^{\prime}\in V_{v}

such that

(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})∈E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.
Since
V_{u}
and
V_{v}
are strongly connected sets,
there is a path from
φ(u)
to
φ(v)

using only vertices of
V_{u}\cup V_{v}
.
Thus,
φ
is an
H
-routing in
D
.

Observation 6.3
.

If a digraph
D
contains
H
as a butterfly minor, then it also contains an
H
-routing.

Proof 6.7
.

For each vertex
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, let
V_{v}
be the set of vertices of
D
which were butterfly contracted into
v
.
By definition of butterfly contraction, there is some vertex
u_{v}\in V_{v}
such that, for all
x\in V_{v}
,
u_{v}
can reach
x
or
x
can reach
u_{v}
.
We then define
φ(v)=u_{v}
for every
v∈V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
.
The fact that
φ
is an
H
-routing in
D
follows from the fact that the sets
V_{v}
defined above are pairwise vertex-disjoint and the paths connecting
V_{v}
to
V_{u}
for each arc
(v,u)
in
H
do not intersect other sets
V_{w}
if
v\neq w\neq u
.

To see that
H
-routings are distinct from the previous containment relations, consider the bidirected star with
k
leaves.
It contains a
{\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

as a strong immersion, butterfly minor or strong minor.

We now consider how to apply
H
-routings in our setting.
Let us briefly consider the following statement proved by Leaf and Seymour for undirected graphs to motivate our next results.

Lemma 6.8

(
[
23
, statement 2.3]
)
.

Let
r\geq 1
and
h\geq 3
be integers, let
G
be a connected graph with
|V\mathchoice{\left(G\right)}{(G)}{(G)}{(G)}|\geq(r+2)(2h-5)+2
.
Then,
G
contains one of the following

\blacktriangleright

a path with
r
vertices whose internal vertices have degree two in
G
, or

\blacktriangleright

a spanning tree
T
with at least
h
leaves.

In the undirected setting, both cases of the previous lemma can be useful regarding the connectivity they provide:
For every pair of leaves, a tree contains a path connecting them without intersecting any other leaves, whereas a long path with internal vertices of degree two yields can have a useful special meaning if the graph is an auxiliary graph describing the intersections between some objects,
as such a path provide a unique way to traverse the objects corresponding to the vertices of the path.

In the directed setting, however, neither out-trees nor in-trees provide any connectivity between their leaves.
To get a statement that we can use in the sequel, we therefore have to find an alternative for the trees used in
lemma

6.8
.
We first need the following well-known result about acyclic digraphs, often stated in terms of chains and anti-chains in partial orderings.

Observation 6.4
.

Every acyclic digraph
D
with more than
\ell\cdot p
vertices but no
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptp}
as a subgraph contains a set
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
of size
\ell
such that no vertex in
X
can reach any other vertex in
X
.

Proof 6.9
.

For each
i\geq 0
let
L_{i}\coloneqq\{v\in V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\mathrel{:}\text{the longest path from a source to $v$ in $T$}\text{ has}\allowbreak\text{length }i\}
.
As, by assumption,
D
does not contain a path of length
p
,
L_{i}=\emptyset
for all
i\geq p
.
Furthermore, by construction, no vertex
v\in L_{i}
can reach any other vertex
u\in L_{i}
, as otherwise the longest path from a source to
u
would be longer than
i
.

Every vertex of
D
lies in some
L_{i}
.
By the pigeon-hole principle, at least one
L_{i}
must contain at least
\ell
vertices, proving the claim.

In the next
lemma
, we establish a simple base case where we are guaranteed to either find a long path or a
{\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.50691pt}}_{k}
-routing.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
D
be a strongly connected digraph.
Let
s\in V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
be a vertex such that
D-\{s\}
contains at least
kp
strongly connected components.
Then,
D
contains one of the following:

(B1)

a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

.

-routing in

.

Let
v_{i},v_{j}\in X
be two distinct vertices.
Let
C_{t}\in V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
be a sink in
T
which is reachable from
C_{i}
and let
C_{r}\in V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
be a source in
T
which can reach
C_{j}
.

Since
D
is strongly connected and
D-\{s\}
is not, there is some
u_{t}\in V\mathchoice{\left(C_{t}\right)}{(C_{t})}{(C_{t})}{(C_{t})}
and some
u_{r}\in V\mathchoice{\left(C_{r}\right)}{(C_{r})}{(C_{r})}{(C_{r})}
such that
(u_{t},s)
and
(s,u_{r})
are arcs in
D
.

Because
C_{i}
can reach
C_{t}
and
C_{r}
can reach
C_{j}
in
T
, there is a
v_{i}
-
u_{t}
-path
P_{i,t}
and a
u_{r}
-
v_{j}
-path
P_{r,j}
in
D
such that
P_{i,t}
and
P_{r,j}
do not intersect any vertex in
X\setminus\{v_{i},v_{j}\}
.
Hence,
P_{i,t}\cdot(u_{t},s)\cdot(s,u_{s})\cdot P_{r,j}
is a
v_{i}
-
v_{j}
-path in
D
which is disjoint from
X\setminus\{v_{i},v_{j}\}
.

We conclude that
\varphi
is a
{\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.50691pt}}_{k}
-routing in
D
, and so
(B2)
holds, as desired.

We now prove an analogous statement to
lemma

6.8
for directed graphs.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{n}_{\ref{thm:routing star or path}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{n}_{\ref{thm:routing star or path}}}\mathchoice{\left(k,p\right)}{(k,p)}{(k,p)}{(k,p)}\coloneqq 2k^{2}p^{3}
.
Every strongly connected digraph
D
with
|V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}|\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{n}_{\ref{thm:routing star or path}}}\mathchoice{\left(k,p\right)}{(k,p)}{(k,p)}{(k,p)}
contains one of the following:

(Q1)

a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptp}
as a subgraph, or

(Q2)

a
{\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.50691pt}}_{k}
-routing over some
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.

Proof 6.12
.

We show that
(Q2)
holds if
(Q1)
does not hold.

We iterate from
1
to
2kp
, potentially stopping earlier.
On step
i
, we construct a vertex sequence
X_{i}
and a digraph
D_{i}
satisfying all of the following.

(A1)

\mathchoice{\left(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}\right)}{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i})}{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i})}{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i})}=X_{i}
and so
|X_{i}|\geq i
,

(A2)

D_{i}
is strongly connected component of
D_{i-1}-\{v_{i}\}
(and so
v_{i}
is not on
D_{i}
),

(A3)

for every
1\leq j\leq i
,
v_{j}
lies on
D_{j-1}
,

(A4)

V\mathchoice{\left(D_{i}\right)}{(D_{i})}{(D_{i})}{(D_{i})}\geq(2kp-i)kp^{2}
.

Start by setting
X_{0}
as the empty sequence and
D_{0}=D
.
Clearly,
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
and
(A4)
hold for 0 (to simplify notation, we set
D_{-1}\coloneqq D
and replace
\{v_{0}\}
with the empty set so that
(A2)
is well-defined for
i=0
).
On step
i\leq 2kp
, we consider the following cases.

Case 1.
There is a
v\in V(D_{i-1})
such that
D_{i-1}-v
contains at least
kp
strongly connected components.

As we assume that
(Q1)
does not hold, we know from
lemma

6.10
that
(Q2)
holds, and we are done with the construction and the proof.

Case 2.
There is a
v\in V(D_{i-1})
such that
D_{i-1}-v
is strongly connected.

Then set
X_{i}\coloneqq X_{i-1}\cdot(v)
and set
D_{i}\coloneqq D_{i-1}-v
.
It is immediate from the choice of
v
and our assumption on
D_{i-1}-v
that
(A3)
,
(A1)
,
(A2)
and
(A4)
hold for
i
because they hold for
i-1
.

Case 3.
There is a
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(D_{i}\right)}{(D_{i})}{(D_{i})}{(D_{i})}
such that the largest strongly connected component
C
of
D_{i-1}-v
has at least
|V\mathchoice{\left(D_{i-1}\right)}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}|-kp^{2}
many vertices.

Then set
X_{i}\coloneqq X_{i-1}\cdot(v)
and
D_{i}\coloneqq C
.
Note that
|V\mathchoice{\left(D_{i-1}\right)}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}|-kp^{2}\geq(2kp-i)kp^{2}
as
(A4)
holds for
i-1
.
Hence, it is again immediate from the choice of
v
and from
our assumption over
C
that
(A3)
,
(A1)
,
(A2)
and
(A4)
hold for
i
as they also hold for

i-1
.

This completes the case distinction above.
Now, assume towards a contradiction that none of the three cases above apply and
i\leq 2kp
.

For every
v\in V(D_{i-1})
, we know that
D_{i-1}-v
has fewer than
kp
strong components because
Case 1
does not apply.
Further,
D_{i-1}-v
is not strongly connected, as
Case 2
does not apply.
Finally, we know that each strong component of
D_{i-1}-v
has fewer than
|V\mathchoice{\left(D_{i-1}\right)}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}-kp^{2}|
vertices because
Case 3
does not apply.

For each
v\in V(D_{i-1})
, let
\mathcal{C}_{v}
be the set of strong components of
D^{\prime}_{v}\coloneqq D_{i-1}-v
.
Let
A_{v}=\{(u,w)\mathrel{:}u,w\in V(D^{\prime}_{v})
and there is no path from
u
to
w
in
D^{\prime}_{v}\}
.
Let
n_{v}=|V(D^{\prime}_{v})|
.

Now let
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(D_{i-1}\right)}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}{(D_{i-1})}
be arbitrary.
For any two distinct components
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{v}
, there is no path in
D^{\prime}_{v}
from any
u\in V(C_{1})
to any
w\in V(C_{2})
or vice versa.
Thus,
A_{v}
contains all possible arcs from vertices in
C_{1}
to vertices in
C_{2}
or all possible arcs from vertices in
C_{2}
to vertices in
C_{1}
.
Fixing some arbitrary ordering
\mathchoice{\left(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{c}\right)}{(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{c})}{(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{c})}{(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{c})}
of the elements of
\mathcal{C}_{v}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle|A_{v}|\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C_{a},C_{b}\in\mathcal{C}_{v},\\[0.0pt]
a<b\end{subarray}}|V(C_{a})|\cdot|V(C_{b})|.

Let
C\in\mathcal{C}_{v}
be a strong component of
D^{\prime}_{v}
with the maximal number of vertices among all components in
\mathcal{C}_{v}
.
The previous inequality implies that
|A_{v}|\geq|V(C)|\cdot\sum_{C_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{v}\setminus\{C\}}|V(C_{a})|
.

By assumption
|V(C)|\leq n_{v}-kp^{2}-1
, and since the strong components of
D^{\prime}_{v}
form a partition of
D^{\prime}_{v}
, we obtain that
\sum_{C_{a}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C\}}|V(C_{a})|\geq kp^{2}
.
Note that
n_{v}-kp^{2}-1\geq kp^{2}
since
(A4)
holds for
i<2kp
.
Since
D^{\prime}_{v}
contains fewer than
kp
strong components, we also obtain
|V(C)|\geq(n_{v}-1)/kp
.
From the inequality above, we obtain

\displaystyle|A_{v}|

\displaystyle\geq|V(C)|\cdot\sum_{C_{a}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C\}}|V(C_{a})|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n_{v}-1}{kp}\cdot\sum_{C_{a}\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C\}}|V(C_{a})|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n_{v}-1}{kp}\cdot kp^{2}=(n_{v}-1)\cdot p

Hence,
\sum_{v\in V(D_{i-1})}|A_{v}|\geq n_{v}\cdot(n_{v}-1)\cdot p
.
Since
A_{v}
does not contain any reflexive tuples and there are
n_{v}(n_{v}-1)
non-reflexive tuples in the set
V(D_{i-1})\times V(D_{i-1})
, by the pigeon-hole principle we deduce that there are
u,v\in V(D_{i-1})
and there are
v_{1},\dots,v_{p}\in V(D_{i-1})
such that
(u,v)\in A_{v_{j}}
for all
1\leq j\leq p
.
Thus, every path from
u
to
w
in
D_{i-1}
must contain each of the vertices
v_{1},\dots v_{p}
and thus be of length at least
p+1
(by definition,
(u,w)\not\in A_{u}\cup A_{w}
), a contradiction to the assumption that
(Q1)
does not hold, that is, that
D
does not contain a path of length
p
.
Hence, one of the three cases must apply, and we can complete the construction above.

If at any point during the construction we end up at Case 1,
then, as argued above,
(Q2)
is true and we are done.
Otherwise, we know that
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
and
(A4)
hold for
2kp
.
We now show that
(Q2)
holds.

Let
\mathchoice{\left(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{2kp}\right)}{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{2kp})}{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{2kp})}{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{2kp})}\coloneqq X_{2kp}
.
We inductively construct disjoint sets
A_{i},B_{i}\subseteq\{v_{1},\dots,\allowbreak v_{2pk}\}
and we construct for each
v_{j}\in B_{i}
a path
P_{j}^{+}\subseteq D_{j}
from
v_{j}
to
V\mathchoice{\left(D_{2kp}\right)}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}
and a path
P_{j}^{-}\subseteq D_{j}
from
V\mathchoice{\left(D_{2kp}\right)}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}
to
v_{j}
such that
\big(V(P_{j}^{+})\cup V(P_{j}^{-})\big)\cap A_{i}=\emptyset
for all
v_{j}\in B_{i}
.
Finally,
|B_{i}|=i
,
|A_{i}|\geq 2kp-2pi
and the elements of
B_{i}
are contained in
X_{i}
.

We start by setting
A_{0}\coloneqq\{v_{1},\dots,v_{2kp}\}
and
B_{0}\coloneqq\emptyset
, which obviously meet the requirements.
On step
i<k
, let
j
be minimal such that
v_{j}\in A_{i-1}
.

Let
P_{j}^{+}
be a shortest path in
D_{j-1}
from
v_{j}
to a vertex in
D_{2kp}
and let
P_{j}^{-}
be a shortest path from a vertex in
D_{2kp}
to
v_{j}
, again in
D_{j-1}
.
As
D_{j-1}
is strongly connected and
D_{2kp}\subseteq D_{j-1}
due to
(A2)
, such paths exist.
Furthermore,
P_{j}^{+}
and
P_{j}^{-}
are both of length at most
p
and all internal vertices of
P_{j}^{+}
and
P_{j}^{-}
are disjoint from
X_{j-1}
(and so from
B_{i-1}
) due to
(A2)
.

We define
B_{i}=B_{i-1}\cup\{v_{j}\}
and
A_{i}=A_{i-1}\setminus\big(V(P_{j}^{+}\cup P_{j}^{-})\big)
.
As
|V(P_{j}^{+})|,|V(P_{j}^{-})|<p
and
|A_{i-1}|\geq 2kp-2p(i+1)
,
we immediately get that
|A_{i}|\geq 2kp-2pi
, as required.
Clearly, the other conditions are satisfied as well.

The construction stops after
k
steps with a set
B_{k}
such that
for each
v_{j}\in B_{k}
there are paths
P^{+}_{j},P^{-}_{j}
such that
P^{+}_{j}
is a
v_{j}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(D_{2kp}\right)}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}
-path and
P^{-}_{j}
is a
V\mathchoice{\left(D_{2kp}\right)}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}{(D_{2kp})}
-
v
-path and both paths are internally disjoint from
B_{k}
.

Let
\varphi:V\mathchoice{\left({\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.50691pt}}_{k}\right)}{({\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

We show that

{\hbox{\hskip 4.50691pt\hskip-4.50691pt\hbox{$\mathbf{K}$}\hskip-4.50691pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

-routing in
D
.
To see this, let
v_{i},v_{j}\in B_{k}
and let
P_{i,j}
be a path in
D_{2kp}
from the end vertex of
P_{i}^{+}
to the start of

.
Then
P_{i}^{+}\cup P_{i,j}\cup P_{j}^{-}
contains a

-path disjoint from
B_{k}
.
Hence,
(Q2)
holds, concluding the proof of the theorem.

6.2
Finding
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
