\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routings in temporal digraphs

Of particular interest to us are
H
-routings in temporal digraphs
where
H
is just a simple path
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
.
This is natural in our context, as, for example, an acyclic grid is nothing but a
“horizontal” linkage
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q}\right)}{(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q})}
that intersects a sequence
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\{P_{1},\dots,P_{p}\}
of pairwise disjoint digraphs
P_{i}
in the order
P_{1},\dots,P_{p}
where each
P_{i}
happens to be a simple path intersecting the paths in
\mathcal{Q}
in the order
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q}
.

Our next goal, therefore, is to identify properties of the
individual layers of a temporal digraph
T
that guarantee the existence of
a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routing in
T
.

Recall from
section

2
that a digraph
D
is

unilateral
if, for every pair
u,v\in V(D)
of vertices,

v
can reach
u
or
u
can reach
v
.
As proved in
[
15
, Theorem 3.10]
, a digraph
D
is
unilateral if and only if there is a walk visiting all the vertices of
D
.

We need a stronger characterisation of unilateral digraphs for our results, and we need the following classic result of Rédei to prove it.

Theorem 6.12

(
[
31
]
, Theorem 1)
.

Given a tournament
T
, the number of Hamiltonian paths in
T
is odd.

In particular,
theorem

6.6
implies that a tournament always contains at least one Hamiltonian path.

Lemma 6.13
.

A digraph
D
is unilateral if and only if for every
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
there is a walk
W
of length at most
kn
in
D
with
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
, where
k=|S|
and
n=|V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}|
.

Proof 6.14
.

If there is a walk
W
in
D
with
V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}=V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, then
D
is clearly unilateral.

Now assume
D
is unilateral and let
S=\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots v_{k}\}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.
We construct an auxiliary tournament
T
with
V(T)=S
in the following way.
Given vertices
v_{i},v_{j}\in S
, with
i<j
, if there exists in
D
a path
P_{i,j}
in
D
starting in
v_{i}
and ending in
v_{j}
we add the arc
v_{i}v_{j}
to
E(T)
.
Otherwise, there must be a path
P_{j,i}
starting in
v_{j}
and ending in
v_{i}
, and we add the arc
v_{j}v_{i}
to
E(T)
.
From
theorem

6.6
,
T
must contain a Hamiltonian path
P
.
From
P
we construct the wanted walk
W
by replacing the arc
v_{i}v_{j}
of
P
with the path
P_{i,j}
, for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
.
Clearly,
W
is a walk in
D
, and its length is at most
kn
.

Thus, the walk
W_{k}
satisfies the statement of the lemma.

Finding long walks in unilateral digraphs is easy. The task becomes more complicated in temporal digraphs as the connectivity provided by individual layers may differ significantly.
As we show next, one direction of the previous lemma can be retained in the temporal setting.
Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}\in\mathcal{O}(k^{2}n^{kn+2})
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}\coloneqq kn\sum_{i=1}^{kn}n^{i}
.
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}

Let
T
be a temporal digraph with
n
vertices where each layer is unilateral, and let
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
be a set of size
k
.
If
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}
, then
T
contains a temporal walk
W
with
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
.

Proof 6.16
.

By
lemma

6.13
, for each
1\leq i\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
there is a walk
W_{i}
of length at most
kn
in
D_{i}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
such that
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W_{i}\right)}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}
.
Note that there are
\sum_{i=1}^{kn}n^{i}
distinct walks of length at most
kn
over the vertex set of
T
.

As
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\geq kn\sum_{i=1}^{kn}n^{i}
, by the pigeon-hole principle, there is some walk
W^{\prime}
which appears on at least
kn
different layers.
Let
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{kn}
be time steps such that each
D_{t_{i}}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
contains the walk
W^{\prime}
.
Now set
W\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left((v_{i},t_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq kn\text{ and $v_{i}$ is the $i$th vertex on $W^{\prime}$}\right)}{((v_{i},t_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq kn\text{ and $v_{i}$ is the $i$th vertex on $W^{\prime}$})}{((v_{i},t_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq kn\text{ and $v_{i}$ is the $i$th vertex on $W^{\prime}$})}{((v_{i},t_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq kn\text{ and $v_{i}$ is the $i$th vertex on $W^{\prime}$})}
.

Since
V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}=V\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}
, we also have
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
, as desired.

{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

as a temporal subpath.

Let

be a minimal temporal subwalk of

such that

and consider a maximal set

contains a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

.
Therefore, we can assume
|S|\leq k-1
.
Let us denote by

with

the temporal subwalk of

starting in

and ending in

.
Further, we denote by

the subwalk of

starting in

and ending in

, and by

the temporal subwalk of

starting in

and ending in

.

, all vertices of

appear once on

.

.

the temporal subwalk joining the first and second occurrence of

on

.

is minimal,

must contain a vertex appearing only once on

, otherwise the concatenation of the temporal subwalk of

going from

would be a shorter subwalk of

containing the same amount of vertices, a contradiction to the minimality of
W^{\prime}
.
Hence, the subwalks
W^{\prime}_{i}
do not contain repeated vertices.
Since
W
does not contain any
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
as a temporal subpath,
|V(W_{i})|\leq k-1
.
Further, for all

, the internal vertices of

must occur in a subwalk

for some

with

and

.
Since

and
|V(W^{\prime})|\geq\frac{k^{2}}{2}
this is a contradiction.

The previous two lemmas immediately imply the following result.
Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{theorem:one-way connected temporal digraph contains P_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{6}(k,n))
.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{theorem:one-way connected temporal digraph contains P_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,k\right)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}{(n,k)}\coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:temporal one-way connected contains walk with many vertices}}}\mathchoice{\left(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2}\right)}{(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2})}{(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2})}{(n,\frac{k^{2}}{2})}

-routing over

Proof 6.19
.

Let
S^{\prime}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
with
|S^{\prime}|=\frac{k^{2}}{2}
.
By
lemma

6.15
,
T
contains a temporal walk
W
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
.
In particular,
|V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}|\geq\frac{k^{2}}{2}
.
By

6.17
, there is some

such that

routing over

and
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

As discussed at the beginning of the previous subsection, the existence of
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routings in a routing temporal digraph relates to the connectivity provided by the columns of an acyclic grid, which only allows routing from top to bottom and from left to right.
If we consider a fence instead of an acyclic grid, the fence allows us to route upwards as well as downwards, as the columns alternate in direction.
Two consecutive columns taken together allow one to go from any row to any other row and in this way resemble a strongly connected digraph like a cycle
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014ptk}
or a bidirected
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

much more than a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

.
In this section, we aim to find
H
-routings that provide this higher level of connectivity.

We first define

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 6k_{1}(k_{2})^{2}-8k_{1}k_{2}+2k_{1}-2(k_{2})^{2}+3k_{2}

and prove the following technical
lemma
.
Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}\in\mathcal{O}(k_{1}(k_{2})^{2})
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
T
be a temporal digraph, let
W
be a temporal walk in
T
, let
k_{1},k_{2}
be integers, and let
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
be a set of size at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}
.
Then there is some
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
such that one of the following is true:

(R1)

W
contains a
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk_{1}}
-routing over
S^{\prime}
, or

(R2)

there are (possibly arcless) walks
W_{1},W_{a},W_{b},W_{c}
in
D
such that
W_{1}
is a subwalk of
W
leaving and arriving at the same time steps as
W
,
W_{a}\cdot W_{b}\cdot W_{c}=W_{1}
,
W_{a}
and
W_{c}
are internally disjoint from
S^{\prime}
, and
W_{b}
contains a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

Proof 6.21
.

To simplify arithmetic steps, we define
k_{3}=(k_{1}-1)(k_{2}-1)
and
s_{1}=2(k_{3}+k_{2})+k_{2}-1
.
Note that
(k_{2}-1)(s_{1}-1)+k_{2}(4k_{3}+k_{2})=6k_{1}(k_{2})^{2}-8k_{1}k_{2}+2k_{1}-2(k_{2})^{2}+3k_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k_{1},k_{2}\right)}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}{(k_{1},k_{2})}\leq|S|
.

In the following claim, we identify a base case for the proof, which is used several times later.

Claim 2
.

Let
\widehat{W}=\widehat{W}_{a}\cdot\widehat{W}_{b}\cdot\widehat{W}_{c}
be a temporal walk inside
W
such that
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\widehat{W}\right)}{(\widehat{W})}{(\widehat{W})}{(\widehat{W})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\widehat{W}\right)}{(\widehat{W})}{(\widehat{W})}{(\widehat{W})}=\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
and let
\widehat{S}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(\widehat{W}_{a}\right)}{(\widehat{W}_{a})}{(\widehat{W}_{a})}{(\widehat{W}_{a})}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(\widehat{W}_{c}\right)}{(\widehat{W}_{c})}{(\widehat{W}_{c})}{(\widehat{W}_{c})}\cap S
be a set such that each vertex of
\widehat{S}
appears exactly once on
\widehat{W}_{a}
and exactly once on
\widehat{W}_{c}
.
If
|\widehat{S}|\geq k_{3}+1
, then there is some
S^{\prime}\subseteq\widehat{S}
such that
(R1)
or
(R2)
holds.

{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk_{2}}
is mapped to
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W_{b}\right)}{(W_{b})}{(W_{b})}{(W_{b})}
.
Further,
W_{a}
and
W_{c}
are internally disjoint from
S^{\prime}
.
Thus, the temporal walk
W_{1}\coloneqq W_{a}\cdot W_{b}\cdot W_{c}
satisfies
(R2)
.

Let
W^{\prime}
be a minimal temporal subwalk of
W
such that
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}
.
We say that a temporal subwalk
R
of
W
is a
return (around a vertex
u\in S
)
if
u
appears exactly twice on
R
,
R
starts and ends on
u
and all vertices of
(V\mathchoice{\left(R\right)}{(R)}{(R)}{(R)}\cap S)\setminus\{u\}
appear exactly once on
R
.
Note that, by minimality of
W^{\prime}
, each return
R
around a vertex
u
must contain a vertex
u^{\prime}\in S
whose only occurrence on
W^{\prime}
is on
R
.

Let
R
be a return in
W^{\prime}
maximising the cardinality of
S_{1}\coloneqq V\mathchoice{\left(R\right)}{(R)}{(R)}{(R)}\cap S
.
We consider two cases.

Case 1:

|S_{1}|\leq s_{1}-1
.

We decompose
W^{\prime}
into
Q_{1}\cdot R_{1}\cdot Q_{2}\cdot R_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot Q_{x}\cdot R_{x}\cdot Q_{x+1}=W^{\prime}
, where each
Q_{i}
is a temporal walk on which no vertex of
S
appears twice and each
R_{i}
is a return in
W^{\prime}
.
By definition of return, such a decomposition is unique.
We distinguish between two subcases.

Case 1.1:

x\geq k_{2}
.

For each

let

-

which contains every vertex of

appears exactly once in

contains a

-routing over

.

.

Case 1.2:

x<k_{2}
.

Since
|S|\geq(k_{2}-1)(s_{1}-1)+k_{2}(4k_{3}+k_{2})
and
|V\mathchoice{\left(R_{i}\right)}{(R_{i})}{(R_{i})}{(R_{i})}\cap S|\leq|S_{1}|\leq s_{1}-1
for every

, there is some

such that
|V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{b}\right)}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}\cap S|\geq(|S|-(k_{2}-1)(s_{1}-1))/k_{2}\geq 4k_{3}+k_{2}
.

Let

be the time step in which

departs, and let

be the time step in which

arrives.
Let

be a temporal
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}
-

.
We consider three further subcases.

Case 1.2.1:

|S_{b}\setminus(S_{a}\cup S_{c})|\geq k_{2}
.

Let

be a set of size

.

be a shortest walk inside

containing every vertex of

.

be a temporal
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}

and

are internally disjoint from

.

contains a
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

.

Case 1.2.2:

|(S_{b}\cap S_{a})\setminus S_{c}|\geq k_{3}+1
or
|(S_{b}\cap S_{c})\setminus S_{a}|\geq k_{3}+1
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
|(S_{b}\cap S_{a})\setminus S_{c}|\geq k_{3}+1
.
The other case follows analogously.

Let
S_{2}=(S_{b}\cap S_{a})\setminus S_{c}
.
Let
\widehat{W}_{c}=Q_{b}\cdot Q_{c}^{\prime}
.
Each vertex of
\widehat{S}
appears exactly once on
Q_{a}
and exactly once on
\widehat{W}_{c}
.
Hence, by
claim

2
, there is some
S^{\prime}\subseteq S_{2}
such that
(R1)
or
(R2)
holds.

Case 1.2.3:
The conditions of
Case 1.2.1
and
Case 1.2.2
do not apply.

We first show that
|S_{a}\cap S_{c}|\geq k_{3}+1
.
Since
|S_{b}\setminus(S_{a}\cup S_{c})|\leq k_{2}-1
and
|S_{b}|\geq 4k_{3}+k_{2}
, we have that
|S_{b}\cap(S_{a}\cup S_{c})|\geq 4k_{3}+1
.
Hence,
|S_{b}\cap S_{a}|\geq 2k_{3}+1
or
|S_{b}\cap S_{c}|\geq 2k_{3}+1
.

Assume, without loss of generality, that
|S_{b}\cap S_{a}|\geq 2k_{3}+1
holds.
Because
|(S_{b}\cap S_{a})\setminus S_{c}|\leq k_{3}
, we have that
|S_{a}\cap S_{c}|\geq k_{3}+1
, as desired.

Let
S_{2}\subseteq S_{a}\cap S_{c}
be a set of size
k_{3}+1
.
Since
Q_{a}^{\prime}
and
Q_{c}
are temporal paths, each vertex of
S_{2}
appears exactly once on
Q_{a}
and exactly once on
Q_{c}
.
Hence, by
claim

2
, there is some
S^{\prime}\subseteq S_{2}
such that
(R1)
or
(R2)
holds.

Case 2:

|S_{1}|\geq s_{1}
.

Let
Q_{a},Q_{b}
be two temporal paths inside
W^{\prime}
such that
Q_{a}\cdot R\cdot Q_{b}
is a walk starting at
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}

Case 2.2.2:

|V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{a}\right)}{(Q_{a})}{(Q_{a})}{(Q_{a})}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{b}\right)}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}\cap S_{1}|\geq k_{3}+1
.

Let
S_{3}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{a}\right)}{(Q_{a})}{(Q_{a})}{(Q_{a})}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{b}\right)}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}\cap S_{1}
be a set of size
k_{3}+1
.
As
Q_{a}
and
Q_{b}
are temporal paths, each vertex of
S_{3}
appears exactly once in each of those temporal paths.
Hence, by
claim

2
, there is some
S^{\prime}\subseteq S_{3}
such that
(R1)
or
(R2)
holds.

The next lemma allows us to transfer the strong connectivity of each individual layer of a temporal digraph to the temporal digraph as a whole.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
T
be a temporal digraph in which each layer is strongly connected.
If
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\geq|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1
, then every
u\in V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
temporally reaches every
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
.

Proof 6.24
.

Let
u\in V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
and let
n=|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1
.

For each
0\leq i\leq n
let
R_{i}
be the set of vertices of
T
which
u
temporally reaches in at most
i
time steps.
Clearly
u\in R_{0}
and so
|R_{0}|=1
.
Further,
|R_{i}|\leq|R_{j}|
if
i\leq j
.

We show that, for every
0\leq i<n
, if
|R_{i}|<V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
, then
|R_{i+1}|>|R_{i}|
.
Let
R_{i}
be such a set and let
X=V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\setminus V\mathchoice{\left(R_{i}\right)}{(R_{i})}{(R_{i})}{(R_{i})}
.
Since
D_{i+1}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
is strongly connected, there is some
w\in R_{i}
and some
v\in X
such that
(w,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D_{i+1}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\right)}{(D_{i+1}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)})}{(D_{i+1}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)})}{(D_{i+1}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)})}
.
By assumption, there is a temporal walk
W
from
u
to
w
within the first
i
layers in
D
.
Extending
W
with the arc
\mathchoice{\left(w,v\right)}{(w,v)}{(w,v)}{(w,v)}
allows us to obtain a walk from
u
to
v
within the first
i+1
layers.
Hence,
|R_{i+1}|>|R_{i}|
.

Since
n\geq|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}-1|
and
|R_{0}|=1
, we have that
|R_{n}|=n+1
.
Thus,
u
temporally reaches every
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
.

6.4
Making the layers strongly connected

We are almost ready to prove the main result of this part, which allows
us to construct
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014ptk}
- or
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

-routings in temporal digraphs

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
D
be a temporal digraph in which each layer is strongly
connected, let
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, let
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
and let
s\in S
.
If
\ell\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq|S|\cdot(|V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}|-1)
, then
D
contains a
temporal
v
-
s
-walk
W
with
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
.

Proof 6.26
.

Let
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{k}\}\coloneqq S
be an arbitrary ordering of
S
such that
s=s_{k}
, and let
n\coloneqq|V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}|
.
We iteratively construct temporal walks
W_{1},W_{2},\dots,W_{k}
such
that
W_{i}
is a walk from
v
to
s_{i}
within the first
i\cdot(n-1)

layers and
W_{i}
contains
s_{1},\dots,s_{i}
.

Start by taking some temporal
v
-
s_{1}
-walk
W_{1}
within the first
n-1
layers.
By
lemma

6.23
, such a walk exists.

On step
i\geq 2
, let
W^{\prime}
be the temporal
s_{i-1}
-
s_{i}
-walk from
layer
i\cdot(n-1)+1
to layer
(i+1)\cdot(n-1)
in
D
.
By
lemma

6.23
, such a walk exists.
Now set
W_{i+1}=W_{i}\cdot W^{\prime}
.
Since
W_{i}
arrives on
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W_{i}\right)}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}
on time step
i(n-1)

and
W^{\prime}
leaves
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W^{\prime}\right)}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}{(W^{\prime})}
on time step
i(n-1)+1
, we have that

W_{i+1}
is a temporal
v
-
s_{i+1}
-walk as desired.

Thus, the walk
W_{k}
is a temporal
v
-
s
-walk within the first

|S|\cdot(n-1)
layers which contains all vertices of
S
.

-routing for some

in any temporal digraph of sufficiently large lifetime as long as each layer is strongly connected.

We define the following functions:

.

Proof 6.27
.

Let
k_{2}=(k-1)^{2}+1
and let
k_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k,k_{2}\right)}{(k,k_{2})}{(k,k_{2})}{(k,k_{2})}
.
Let
S_{0}\subseteq S
be a set of size
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{lemma:walk with many vertices implies choosable P_k or biP_k routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k,k_{1}\right)}{(k,k_{1})}{(k,k_{1})}{(k,k_{1})}
.
Note that
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\geq(|S_{0}|+k_{1})\cdot(|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1)
.

Let
W_{1}
be a temporal walk of minimal length which contains all vertices of
S_{0}
within the first
|S_{0}|\cdot\mathchoice{\left(|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1\right)}{(|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1)}{(|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1)}{(|V\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}|-1)}
layers of
D
.
By
lemma

6.25
, such a walk
W_{1}
exists.

If
lemma

6.20
(R1)
holds, then
W_{1}

, then

contains a temporal

, we construct the desired temporal path

as follows.

be a temporal

-

-walk in

which is disjoint from

such that

.

Towards this end, we first observe the following.

Observation 6.9
.

Let
A
and
B
be disjoint sets of equal cardinality and let
S
be a sequence containing each element of
A\uplus B
exactly once.
Then there are sequences
S_{1},S_{2}
such that
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}=S
and the following holds

(P1)

S_{1}
starts in
A
and ends in
B
or starts in
B
and ends in
A
, and

(P2)

each of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
contains as many elements of
A
as elements of
B
.

Proof 6.28
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
S
starts at an element of
A
.
The other case follows analogously by swapping
A
and
B
.

Let
S_{1}
be the shortest prefix of
S
containing the same number of elements in
A
and
B
.
Such a prefix exists, as
S
itself is a valid choice.
Since the first element of
S_{1}
lies on
A
, its last element must lie on
B
.
If this were not the case, then
S_{1}
would contain a prefix with more elements of
B
than elements of
A
, which implies that
S_{1}
also contains a shorter prefix with as many elements of
A
as elements of
B
, a contradiction to the choice of
S_{1}
.
Hence,
(P1)
holds.

Let
S_{2}
be such that
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}=S
.
Since both
S_{1}
and
S
contain as many elements of
A
as elements of
B
, we have that
S_{2}
also contains as many elements of
A
as elements of
B
.
Thus,
(P2)
holds.

Note that in
observation

6.9
if
S
does not start and end in an element of the same set, then we can always take
S_{1}
to be
S
and
S_{2}
to be empty.

The next observation is used when obtaining well-linkedness in the case of a
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

-routing.

Observation 6.10
.

Let
C
be a directed cycle and let
f:V\mathchoice{\left(C\right)}{(C)}{(C)}{(C)}\to\mathds{Z}
be a function such that
\sum_{v\in V\mathchoice{\left(C\right)}{(C)}{(C)}{(C)}}f(v)=0
.
Then there is some
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(C\right)}{(C)}{(C)}{(C)}
such that for every subpath
P
of
C
starting at
v
we have
\sum_{v\in V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}}f(v)\geq 0
.

Proof 6.29
.

The statement is true if
f(v)=0
for all
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(C\right)}{(C)}{(C)}{(C)}
.
So assume otherwise and take a subpath
P
of
C
minimising
\sum_{v\in V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}}f(v)
.
Note that the weight of this path is negative and that
P
is a proper subpath of
C
.
Let
u
be the vertex on
C
after
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}
.
We claim that
u
has the desired property.
Suppose not, and let
P^{\prime}
be a negative subpath of
C
starting in
u
.
If
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(P^{\prime}\right)}{(P^{\prime})}{(P^{\prime})}{(P^{\prime})}\notin V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}
, then
P\cdot P^{\prime}
is a proper subpath of
C
with lower weight than
P
, a contradiction.
Thus
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(P^{\prime}\right)}{(P^{\prime})}{(P^{\prime})}{(P^{\prime})}\in V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}
.
As
P
was chosen to minimise
\sum_{v\in V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}}f(v)
, the subpath
P\cap P^{\prime}
cannot be of positive weight, and thus we obtain a contradiction to
C
being of total weight 0.

We obtain well-linkedness by progressively “moving” the paths around the

or

Lemma 6.30
.

.
If there is some

of

in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}

Proof 6.31
.

Let
A\subseteq\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
and
B\subseteq\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
be sets with
n\coloneqq|A|=|B|
.
Let
\varphi
be the
R
-routing over
\mathcal{L}
in each
T_{i}
.
We construct an
A
-
B
-linkage
\mathcal{Q}
as follows.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{A}=\{L\in\mathcal{L}\mid\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}\subseteq A\}
and let
\mathcal{L}^{B}=\{L\in\mathcal{L}\mid\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}\subseteq B\}
.
Let
\varphi_{A,B}={\varphi}_{|\mathcal{L}^{A}\cup\mathcal{L}^{B}}
.
Let
\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\}\coloneqq A
and let
\{b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{n}\}\coloneqq B
.

We start by constructing temporal walks
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{W_{1},W_{2},\dots,W_{n}\}
in
T
and by constructing sets
X_{0},\dots,X_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{A}
and
Y_{0},\dots,Y_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{B}
such that, for each
0\leq i\leq n
, we have

(W1)

|X_{i}|=i=|Y_{i}|
,

(W2)

W_{i}
is a temporal walk in
T_{i}
which is disjoint from
(\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i})\cup Y_{i-1}
, and

(W3)

\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{W}\right)}{(\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{W})}=\mathcal{L}^{A}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{W}\right)}{(\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{W})}=\mathcal{L}^{B}
.

We consider two cases.

Case 1:

-routing in each

.
Partition
V\mathchoice{\left(R^{\prime}\right)}{(R^{\prime})}{(R^{\prime})}{(R^{\prime})}
into a sequence of subpaths
\mathcal{Q}=\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{x}\right)}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{x})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{x})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{x})}
of
R^{\prime}
where each

can be decomposed into
Q_{i}^{a}\cdot Q_{i}^{b}
such that
|V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{a}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{a})}{(Q_{i}^{a})}{(Q_{i}^{a})}|\geq 1
,
|V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{b}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{b})}{(Q_{i}^{b})}{(Q_{i}^{b})}|\geq 1
,
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{a}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{a})}{(Q_{i}^{a})}{(Q_{i}^{a})})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{A}
and
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{b}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{b})}{(Q_{i}^{b})}{(Q_{i}^{b})})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{B}
.
Since
\varphi(V\mathchoice{\left(R^{\prime}\right)}{(R^{\prime})}{(R^{\prime})}{(R^{\prime})})=\mathcal{L}^{A}\cup\mathcal{L}^{B}
, such a decomposition exists.
Now define the function
f:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathds{Z}
with
f(Q_{i}=Q_{i}^{a}\cdot Q_{i}^{b})=|\varphi(Q_{i}^{a})\cap\mathcal{L}^{A}|-|\varphi(Q_{i}^{b})\cap\mathcal{L}^{B}|
.

From
observation

6.10
we know there is some
v\in V\mathchoice{\left(R^{\prime}\right)}{(R^{\prime})}{(R^{\prime})}{(R^{\prime})}
for which every subpath
P
of
R^{\prime}
starting at

satisfies
|\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})\cap\mathcal{L}^{A}|\geq|\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})\cap\mathcal{L}^{B}|
.
Let
Q
be the subpath of
R^{\prime}
starting at
v
and containing every vertex of
R^{\prime}
, and let
\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{n}\}\coloneqq V\mathchoice{\left(Q\right)}{(Q)}{(Q)}{(Q)}
be an ordering of the vertices of
Q
according to their occurrence along
Q
.

We iteratively construct the desired walks
W_{i}
and sets

such that, for each

,

\varphi_{A,B}(\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1}^{i}\right)}{(Q_{1}^{i})}{(Q_{1}^{i})}{(Q_{1}^{i})})\in\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i}
,

(C2)

\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{2}^{i}\right)}{(Q_{2}^{i})}{(Q_{2}^{i})}{(Q_{2}^{i})}\setminus\{\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{2}^{i}\right)}{(Q_{2}^{i})}{(Q_{2}^{i})}{(Q_{2}^{i})}\})\subseteq(\mathcal{L}^{B}\cup X_{i})\setminus Y_{i}
,
|V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{2}^{i}\right)}{(Q_{2}^{i})}{(Q_{2}^{i})}{(Q_{2}^{i})}\cap(\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i})|\geq 1
, and

(C3)

Y_{i}\subseteq\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{3}^{i}\right)}{(Q_{3}^{i})}{(Q_{3}^{i})}{(Q_{3}^{i})})
and
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{3}^{i}\right)}{(Q_{3}^{i})}{(Q_{3}^{i})}{(Q_{3}^{i})})\subseteq Y_{i}\cup X_{i}
.

Start by setting
X_{0}\coloneqq\emptyset
and
Y_{0}\coloneqq\emptyset
.
By choice of
Q
,
(C1)
,
(C2)
,
(C3)
and
(W1)
hold for

On step

(C1)
,
(C2)
and

for

Let

Let

is an

-routing in

The walk

We now show that

Clearly

holds for

If

As

holds for

After

holds by choice of

R^{\prime}-\varphi_{A,B}^{-1}(Y_{i})
, the set
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Z_{i}\right)}{(Z_{i})}{(Z_{i})}{(Z_{i})})
contains as many elements of
\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i}
as elements of
\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i}
.

Start by setting
X_{0}\coloneqq\emptyset
and
Y_{0}\coloneqq\emptyset
.
Clearly,
(W1)
and
(P)
hold for
i=0
.

On step
1\leq i\leq n
, let
Z
be a strong component (and hence a subpath) of
R^{\prime}-\varphi_{A,B}^{-1}(X_{i-1})
such that
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Z\right)}{(Z)}{(Z)}{(Z)})
contains at least one element of
\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1}
and one element of
\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}
.
Since
(W1)
and
(P)
hold for
i-1
, such a strong component exists.

Because
Z
is a bidirected path, it induces a sequence over the elements of
\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1}
and of
\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}
.
Let
Z^{\prime}
be the shortest subpath of
Z
satisfying
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Z^{\prime}\right)}{(Z^{\prime})}{(Z^{\prime})}{(Z^{\prime})})\cap((\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1})\cup(\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}))=\varphi(V\mathchoice{\left(Z\right)}{(Z)}{(Z)}{(Z)})\cap((\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1})\cup(\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}))
.
By
observation

6.9
, there is a subpath
Z^{\prime\prime}
of
Z^{\prime}
starting at one of the endpoints of
Z^{\prime}
such that one endpoint of
Z^{\prime\prime}
is in
\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1}
and the other is in
\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}
, and both
Z^{\prime\prime}
and the rest of
Z^{\prime}
contain as many elements of
\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1}
as they contain elements of
\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}
.
Let
Z^{\prime\prime}
be the shortest such subpath of
Z^{\prime}
.

Let
\{z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{j}\}
be the vertices of
Z^{\prime\prime}
sorted according to their occurrence along
Z^{\prime\prime}
.
Without loss of generality, we have
\varphi_{A,B}(z_{1})\in\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus X_{i-1}
and
\varphi_{A,B}(z_{j})\in\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus Y_{i-1}
.

Let
j_{a}
be the smallest index such that
\varphi_{A,B}(z_{j_{a}})\in\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1}
.
We set
W_{i}
as a temporal
\varphi_{A,B}(z_{j_{a}})
-
\varphi_{A,B}(z_{1})
-walk in
T_{i}
which is disjoint from
(\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1})\cup Y_{i-1}
.
By choice of
j_{a}
and because
\varphi_{A,B}
is an
R^{\prime}
-routing over
\mathcal{L}^{A}\cup\mathcal{L}^{B}
in
T_{i}
and
Z
is a component of
R^{\prime}-\varphi_{A,B}(X_{i-1})
, such a walk
W_{i}
exists.

We set
X_{i}=X_{i-1}\cup\{\varphi_{A,B}(z_{j_{a}})\}
and
Y_{i}=Y_{i-1}\cup\{\varphi_{A,B}(z_{1})\}
.
The vertex
z_{1}
is an endpoint of
Z^{\prime\prime}
,
(P)
holds for
i-1
and
\varphi_{A,B}(V\mathchoice{\left(Z^{\prime\prime}\right)}{(Z^{\prime\prime})}{(Z^{\prime\prime})}{(Z^{\prime\prime})}\setminus\{z_{1}\})
contains one less vertex of
\mathcal{L}^{B}\setminus Y_{i-1}
and one less vertex of
\mathcal{L}^{A}\setminus X_{i-1}
when compared to
Z
.
Hence,
(P)
holds for
i
.
Further,
|X_{i}|=|X_{i-1}|+1=|Y_{i-1}|+1=|Y_{i}|
, and so
(W1)
holds.

This completes the case distinction above and the construction of
W_{1},W_{2},\dots,W_{n}
.
We construct an
A
-
B
-linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
as follows.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
L_{i}^{a}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W_{i}\right)}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}
,
L^{b}_{i}=\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W_{i}\right)}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}{(W_{i})}
,
a_{i}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}^{a}\right)}{(L_{i}^{a})}{(L_{i}^{a})}{(L_{i}^{a})}
and
b_{i}=\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}^{b}\right)}{(L_{i}^{b})}{(L_{i}^{b})}{(L_{i}^{b})}
.
We construct a path
Q_{i}=Q_{i}^{a}\cdot Q_{i}^{t}\cdot Q_{i}^{b}
such that
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{a}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{a})}{(Q_{i}^{a})}{(Q_{i}^{a})}\subseteq L_{i}^{a}-\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\{W_{j}\mid s_{i+1}\leq j\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\}\right)}{(\{W_{j}\mid s_{i+1}\leq j\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\})}{(\{W_{j}\mid s_{i+1}\leq j\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\})}{(\{W_{j}\mid s_{i+1}\leq j\leq\ell\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\})}
,
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{t}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{t})}{(Q_{i}^{t})}{(Q_{i}^{t})}\subseteq\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\{H_{j}\mid s_{i}\leq j\leq s_{i+1}\}\right)}{(\{H_{j}\mid s_{i}\leq j\leq s_{i+1}\})}{(\{H_{j}\mid s_{i}\leq j\leq s_{i+1}\})}{(\{H_{j}\mid s_{i}\leq j\leq s_{i+1}\})}
and
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{b}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{b})}{(Q_{i}^{b})}{(Q_{i}^{b})}\subseteq L_{i}^{b}-\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\{W_{j}\mid s_{1}\leq j\leq s_{i}\}\right)}{(\{W_{j}\mid s_{1}\leq j\leq s_{i}\})}{(\{W_{j}\mid s_{1}\leq j\leq s_{i}\})}{(\{W_{j}\mid s_{1}\leq j\leq s_{i}\})}
.

Since
(W2)
holds, each arc of
W_{i}
corresponds to some path in
D
which is disjoint from
\{L_{j}^{a}\mid i<j\leq n\}\cup\{L_{j}^{b}\mid 1\leq j<i\}
.
Furthermore,
W_{i}
corresponds to some path
Q_{i}^{t}
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\{W_{j}\mid t_{i}\leq j\leq t_{i+1}\}\right)}{(\{W_{j}\mid t_{i}\leq j\leq t_{i+1}\})}{(\{W_{j}\mid t_{i}\leq j\leq t_{i+1}\})}{(\{W_{j}\mid t_{i}\leq j\leq t_{i+1}\})}
.

We set
Q_{i}^{a}
as the subpath of
L_{i}^{a}
ending at
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{t}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{t})}{(Q_{i}^{t})}{(Q_{i}^{t})}
and we set
Q_{i}^{b}
as the subpath of
L_{i}^{b}
starting at
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{i}^{t}\right)}{(Q_{i}^{t})}{(Q_{i}^{t})}{(Q_{i}^{t})}
.
By construction,
Q_{i}\coloneqq Q_{i}^{a}\cdot Q_{i}^{t}\cdot Q_{i}^{b}
is an
a_{i}
-
b_{i}
-path in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\cup\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{W}\right)}{(\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{W})}
which is disjoint from all
Q_{j}
for
1\leq j<i
.
Hence,
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq\{Q_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
is an
A
-
B
-linkage as desired.

Because we can construct such an
A
-
B
-linkage for any choice of
A,B
, we have that
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{W}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{W})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{W})}
, as desired.

We can now show how to obtain well-linkedness from the routing temporal digraph of some linkage
\mathcal{L}
.
The idea is to use
theorem

6.8
to obtain many
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

-routings.
With the pigeon-hole principle, we get many equal routings.
We then use
Observations

6.9
and
6.10
in each case to argue that certain sets are well-linked.
We start by defining

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k\right)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}\cdot 2k\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}}{k}k!.

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\mathcal{O}(k^{11})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{2}(k))
.
Using the pigeon-hole principle, we can combine
theorem

6.8
and
lemma

6.30
to obtain the desired statement.

Proposition 6.32
.

Let
k
be an integer,
h\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{h}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
,
D
be a digraph,
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage of order
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
in
D
and
T
be the routing temporal digraph of
\mathcal{L}
through
\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\{H_{1},\dots,H_{h}\}
, where each
H_{i}
is a subgraph of
D
.
If each layer
D_{i}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
is strongly connected, then there exists some
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
of order
k
such that
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}{(\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{H})}
.

Proof 6.33
.

Let
k_{1}=2k\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}}{k}k!
.
Define
s_{1}=1
and for each
1\leq i\leq k_{1}
define
s_{i}=(i-1)\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k\right)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}+1
.
For each
1\leq i\leq k_{1}
let
T_{i}
be the temporal subgraph of
T
from time step
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}-1
.
Note that
\ell\mathchoice{\left(T_{i}\right)}{(T_{i})}{(T_{i})}{(T_{i})}=s_{i+1}-s_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k\right)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}{({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)},k)}
and that
|\mathcal{L}|=|V\mathchoice{\left(T_{i}\right)}{(T_{i})}{(T_{i})}{(T_{i})}|={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{lemma:routing-implies-well-linked}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
.

By
theorem

6.8
each
T_{i}
contains a
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014ptk}
-routing
\varphi_{i}
or a
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.87 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{1.35pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 1.87 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routing
\varphi_{i}
over some set
\mathcal{L}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
of size
k
.
As there are
k_{1}=2k\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{s}_{\ref{theorem:strongly connected temporal digraph contains H routing}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}}{k}k!
temporal digraphs
T_{i}
, there is some
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
of size
k
and some
H\in\{{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014ptk},{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}\}
such that, for every
i,j\in I
, both
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
have an
H
-routing
\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{j}
over
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{i}=\mathcal{L}_{j}
.
By
lemma

6.30
,
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
is well-linked to
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}\cup\mathcal{H}
.

7
Paths of order-linked sets and acyclic grids

The previous section contains some discussion of the similarities between
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.30139pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 8.76 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.29999pt} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
-routings in routing temporal digraphs and acyclic grids without yet constructing such a grid or any grid-like structure.
We now develop a more abstract framework to model these intuitive observations.
This enables us to lift specific properties of acyclic grids to a more abstract setting.
The techniques and results we develop in this section play an important role both in obtaining the path of well-linked sets, as in
theorem

9.2
, and also in our proof of the Directed Grid Theorem in
[
17
]
.

Figure 7
:
An illustration of
r
-shifts in acyclic grids.
The
2
-shift
(b_{2},b_{3},b_{4})
of
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{4})
is routable in the grid as illustrated here.
However the
3
-shift
(b_{2},b_{3},b_{4})
of
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
is not routable in the grid.

To motivate the following definitions, consider the acyclic grid illustrated in
figure

7
.
Suppose we want to connect some vertex
a_{i}
on the left of the grid to a vertex
b_{j}
on the right.
As in an acyclic grid we can never route upwards,
connecting
a_{i}
to
b_{j}
is possible if and only if
i\leq j
.

Let
A
be the ordered set containing the left-most vertices of the grid, i.e.
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{4}\}
in the example in
figure

7
, ordered from top to bottom and let
B
be the ordered set containing the vertices at the right, i.e.
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{4}\}
in the example, again ordered from top to bottom.

Suppose we are given a subset
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and an equal-sized subset
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
.
Under what conditions can we connect
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
by a linkage
\mathcal{L}
in the grid?
As discussed, we can only connect
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
to
b_{j}\in B^{\prime}
if
i\leq j
.
Furthermore, as the grid is planar, if
\{a_{i_{1}},\dots,a_{i_{l}}\}
are the vertices of
A^{\prime}
ordered by their order in
A
and likewise
\{b_{j_{1}},\dots,b_{j_{l}}\}
are the ordered vertices of
B^{\prime}
, then we have to connect
a_{i_{s}}
to
b_{j_{s}}
, for all
1\leq s\leq l
.
This implies that
i_{s}\leq j_{s}
for all
1\leq s\leq l
.
But even if
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
satisfy this condition, it may still be impossible to connect
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
.
As the example in
figure

7
demonstrates, there may not be enough columns in the grid to route all paths downwards that connect pairs
a_{i_{s}},b_{j_{s}}
with
i_{s}<j_{s}
.
So we may have to restrict the number of pairs
(a_{i_{s}},b_{j_{s}})
for which we allow
i_{s}<j_{s}
.

This idea is formalised in the next definition by the concept of
r
-shifts
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
A=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
and
B=(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
be ordered sets.
Let
r\in\mathds{N}
, let
A^{\prime}
be an ordered subset of
A
and let
B^{\prime}
be an ordered subset of
B
such that
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|
.
We say that
B^{\prime}
is an
r
-shift of
A^{\prime}
if there is a bijection
\pi:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
such that

1.

for all
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
we have that
\pi(a_{i})=b_{j}
implies
i\leq j
;

2.

there are at most
r
vertices
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
with
\pi(a_{i})\neq b_{i}
; and

3.

\pi
is order preserving, that is, for all
a_{i},a_{j}\in A^{\prime}
, if
a_{i}\leq_{A}a_{j}
, then
\pi(a_{i})\leq_{B}\pi(a_{j})
.

In the example of
figure

7
,
(b_{2},b_{3},b_{4})
is a
2
-shift of
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{4})
and
a
3
-shift of
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
.
We are interested in finding pairs of equal-sized ordered sets
A
and
B
inside a digraph D for which,
given a subset
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
,
we can find, inside
D
, a linkage from
A^{\prime}
to any possible
r
-shifts
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
of
A^{\prime}
.
This property is formalised in the following definition.
Recall from
section

2
that we may consider a linkage
\mathcal{L}
as a function
\mathcal{L}\mathrel{:}\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\rightarrow\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
where
\mathcal{L}(a)
is the endpoint of the path in
\mathcal{L}
starting at
a
.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
H
be a digraph, let
A=\mathchoice{\left(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\right)}{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})}{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})}{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})},B=\mathchoice{\left(b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\right)}{(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})}{(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})}{(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
be ordered sets, and let
r\in\mathds{N}
.
We say that
A
is
r
-
order-linked
to
B
in
H
if for every
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and every
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
with
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|
where
B^{\prime}
is an
r
-shift of
A^{\prime}
witnessed by the bijection
\pi
there is an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}
in
H
satisfying
\pi\mathchoice{\left(a\right)}{(a)}{(a)}{(a)}=\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\left(a\right)}{(a)}{(a)}{(a)}
for all
a\in A^{\prime}
.

For (unordered) sets
A,B\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
, we say that
A
is
r
-
order-linked
to
B
in
H
if there exist orderings
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
of
A
and
B
, respectively, such that
A_{1}
is
r
-
order-linked
to
B_{1}
in
H
.

To give an example, it is easily seen that, in any acyclic
(r,r)
-grid
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
, the set
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
is
r
-order-linked to
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
.

We now define a new type of structure which can be seen as an abstraction of acyclic grids.

Definition 7.3

(path of
r
-order-linked sets)
.

A
path of
r
-order-linked sets
of width
w
and length
\ell
is a tuple
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
such that

1.

\mathcal{S}
is a sequence of
\ell+1
pairwise disjoint subgraphs
\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell}\right)}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}
, which are called
clusters
,

2.

for every
0\leq i\leq\ell
there are disjoint sets
A(S_{i}),B(S_{i})\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(S_{i}\right)}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}
of size
w
such that
A(S_{i})
is
r
-order-linked to
B(S_{i})
in
S_{i}
,

3.

\mathscr{P}
is a sequence of
\ell
pairwise disjoint linkages
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}
such that, for every
0\leq i<\ell
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is a
B(S_{i})
-
A(S_{i+1})
-linkage of order
w
which is internally disjoint from
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
and disjoint from every
S\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{S_{i},S_{i+1}\}
.

By definition, for each
1\leq i\leq\ell
there are orderings
\leq_{A(S_{i})}
of
A(S_{i})
and
\leq_{B(S_{i})}
of
B(S_{i})
witnessing the
r
-order-linkedness of
A(S_{i})
and
B(S_{i})
in
S_{i}
.

We say that
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
is
uniform
if for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
we can choose orderings
\leq_{A(S_{i})}
and
\leq_{B(S_{i})}
witnessing that
A(S_{i})
is
r
-order-linked to
B(S_{i})
so that for all
0\leq i<\ell
and all
b_{1},b_{2}\in B(S_{i})
: if
b_{1}\leq_{B(S_{i})}b_{2}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{i}(b_{1})\leq_{A(S_{i+1})}\mathcal{P}_{i}(b_{2})
.

The following notation is used frequently later on.
Given a path of
r
-order-linked sets
(\mathcal{S}\coloneqq(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P}\coloneqq(\mathcal{P}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1}))
and indices
0\leq i\leq j\leq\ell
, we define
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})[i,j]
as the path of
r
-order-linked sets from cluster
i
to cluster
j
.
That is,
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})[i,j]\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left((S_{i},\dots,S_{j}),(\mathcal{P}_{i},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{j-1})\right)}{((S_{i},\dots,S_{j}),(\mathcal{P}_{i},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{j-1}))}{((S_{i},\dots,S_{j}),(\mathcal{P}_{i},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{j-1}))}{((S_{i},\dots,S_{j}),(\mathcal{P}_{i},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{j-1}))}
.

To give an example, let
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
be an acyclic
(r,r^{2})
-grid, where
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq(Q_{1}^{1},\dots,Q_{1}^{r},Q_{2}^{1},\dots
,
Q_{2}^{r},\dots,Q_{r}^{r})
.
Then
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
contains a path of
r
-order-linked sets as follows.
The cluster
S_{i}
is obtained as the union of the columns
Q_{i}^{j}
, for
1\leq j\leq r
, and, for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
, the subpath
P^{i}
of
P
starting at the first vertex of
P
on
Q_{i}^{1}
and ending at the last vertex of
P
on
Q_{i}^{r}
.
We define
A(S_{i})\coloneqq\{\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(P^{i}\right)}{(P^{i})}{(P^{i})}{(P^{i})}\mathrel{:}P\in\mathcal{P}\}
and
B(S_{i})\coloneqq\{\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(P^{i}\right)}{(P^{i})}{(P^{i})}{(P^{i})}\mathrel{:}P\in\mathcal{P}\}
.
Finally, the linkages
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, for
0\leq i<r
, are obtained by taking the subpaths of the rows connecting
S_{i}
to
S_{i+1}
in the obvious way.
Then
\mathchoice{\left((S_{0},\dots,S_{r}),(\mathcal{P}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{r-1})\right)}{((S_{0},\dots,S_{r}),(\mathcal{P}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{r-1}))}{((S_{0},\dots,S_{r}),(\mathcal{P}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{r-1}))}{((S_{0},\dots,S_{r}),(\mathcal{P}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{r-1}))}
is a path of
r
-order-linked sets.

As the example shows, we can easily obtain a path of
r
-order linked sets from a sufficiently large acyclic grid.
Our next goal is to show that the converse is also true, albeit with bigger bounds.

We first observe the following.

Observation 7.1
.

Let
D=(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})},\mathscr{P})
be a
path of
0
-order-linked sets of width
w
.
For every
0\leq i<j\leq\ell
, every
A^{\prime}\in\{A(S_{i}),B(S_{i})\}
, and
every
B^{\prime}\in\{A(S_{j}),B(S_{j})\}
there is an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}

of order
w
in
D
.
Furthermore, for all
i<k<j
every path in
\mathcal{L}
must intersect
A(S_{k})
and
B(S_{k})
.

Proof 7.4
.

We show the case where
A^{\prime}=A(S_{i})
and
B^{\prime}=B(S_{j})
.
The other cases follow analogously.

For each
i\leq t\leq j-1
construct sets
A_{t},B_{t}
and a linkage
\mathcal{L}_{t}
as follows.
Start by setting
A_{i-1}=A^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{L}_{i-1}
as the linkage containing only the vertices of
A^{\prime}
.

On step
t
, let
B_{t}
be a 0-shift of
A_{t-1}
and let
\mathcal{R}_{t}
be an
A_{t-1}
-
B_{t}
-linkage of order
w
in
S_{t}
.
Since
A(S_{t})
is
0
-order-linked to
B(S_{t})
, such a linkage
\mathcal{R}_{t}
exists.
Let
\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{t}
be the set of paths with
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime})}=\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}_{t}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}_{t})}{(\mathcal{R}_{t})}{(\mathcal{R}_{t})}
.
Now set
A_{t}=\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime})}
and set
\mathcal{L}_{t}=\mathcal{L}_{t-1}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{t}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{t}^{\prime}
.

It is immediate from the construction that
\mathcal{L}_{j}
is an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage of order
w
, as desired.

We are now ready to show that every path of
1
-order-linked sets
contains an acyclic grid. Here we make use of our framework of

H
-routings in temporal digraphs.
The idea is to use the

{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
